English for Work · Customers & creative work

Retail Associate and Cashier English

Help shoppers find suitable items, explain labels and prices, handle checkout and returns, clarify local stock, and give accurate shop-floor handovers.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Read the situation.
Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For Retail sales associates, cashiers, fitting-room attendants, and shop-floor customer service staff.

Open learner book (PDF) ↗

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Use a reference, material, and size to identify a product before making an unverified local-stock claim.
  • Compare stated garment features and care requirements without inventing comfort, fit, or durability claims.
  • Explain a quantity condition, distinguish average and single-item prices, and respect the shopper choice.
  • Correct a duplicate before payment and distinguish the revised basket total from a refund or completed transaction.
  • Apply supplied exchange conditions while separating eligibility from replacement availability and completed processing.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All stores, shoppers, prices, product references, balances, offers, and policies are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not legal advice, payment-system operation, accounting investigation, or accessibility certification. Follow actual store procedures, product information, payment-security requirements, applicable consumer and accessibility laws, and emergency arrangements. A fictional exchange policy does not replace statutory rights. Ask before providing personal assistance or handling a shopper's belongings or mobility aid.

01Finding the item the shopper actually meansWhich blue shirt?+

Your goal

Use a reference, material, and size to identify a product before making an unverified local-stock claim.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Which blue shirt?

Shopper Casey asks associate Hana for the blue shirt in an advertisement. The advertisement contains two blue shirts: linen L214 and cotton C218. Casey wants the cotton version in medium, not the linen version or another size. Local availability has not been checked. Hana confirms C218, blue, medium, and cotton before beginning a stock inquiry. No item has been located, reserved, or sold, and the advertisement does not prove that the requested variation is available at this branch.

Casey: Shopper / Hana: Retail associate

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which product does Casey mean?

Answer and explanation

B. Cotton C218

Casey identifies the cotton version, whose reference is C218.

2. Which size is requested?

Answer and explanation

B. Medium

Medium is the explicit size preference and should not be substituted without agreement.

3. What is known about local stock?

Answer and explanation

D. It has not been checked

The conversation identifies the item before any local availability check has occurred.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The exact request is blue cotton C218 in medium. Linen L214 is a different item even though it is also blue. Confirming the product does not establish stock, a reservation, or permission to substitute another variation.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
item reference
Identifier used to distinguish a product.confirm the item reference
stock-keeping unit
Internal identifier for a stocked product variation, often abbreviated SKU.check the stock-keeping unit
barcode
Machine-readable code used to identify an item or record.scan the barcode
product variation
Specific version differing in size, color, or another feature.confirm the product variation
colorway
Color version offered within a product range.identify the blue colorway
size
Labelled dimension category such as medium.confirm the requested size
cotton
Textile fiber identified for the requested shirt in this case.identify the cotton version
linen
Different textile fiber used in the other advertised shirt.distinguish the linen version
fiber content
Information describing the fibers used in a textile.check the fiber content
advertised item
Product featured in a promotion or advertisement.identify the advertised item
product image
Picture of a product, not proof of local stock.compare the product image
range
Group of related products or variations.identify the product range
local availability
Whether the exact variation is available at the relevant branch.check local availability
branch
Particular store location within a retailer.identify the branch
shop floor
Customer-accessible area where merchandise is displayed.check the shop floor
stockroom
Storage area for merchandise not on the selling floor.check the stockroom
display sample
Item shown for viewing, not necessarily available for sale.distinguish a display sample
stock inquiry
Request to check whether a specific item is available.make a stock inquiry
stock record
System record of recorded merchandise quantities or status.consult the stock record
physical check
Verification of the actual item and its location.request a physical check
reservation
Confirmed arrangement to hold an item under the store process.distinguish a reservation
substitution
Replacement with a different item or variation requiring agreement.discuss a substitution
specification
Set of details defining the requested item.repeat the specification
planogram
Diagram assigning products and display space on a fixture.follow the planogram

A stock-keeping unit, item reference, and barcode may identify different levels of detail in a retailer's system. Use the actual system correctly and read back the shopper-facing details; do not assume a familiar-looking code proves the size or local availability.

C Notice the language

Clarification is part of helping

A shopper may reasonably remember the image rather than an item number. Narrow the choice with a short concrete question, then read back the product details. Avoid making the shopper repeat the whole story or treating a displayed advertisement as proof of local availability.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Narrow the advertisement
The ad shows two blue shirts; do you mean cotton or linen?
Ask for the size
Which size are you looking for?
Name the selected reference
The cotton shirt is C218.
Distinguish the other item
L214 is the linen version.
Read back the request
Blue cotton C218 in medium; is that right?
Separate identification and stock
That's the right shirt; let me check whether we have your size here.
Avoid an advertisement assumption
The advertisement does not confirm stock at this branch.
Confirm before searching
I will use that exact reference and size for the stock inquiry.
Keep alternatives optional
I would ask before offering a different size or material.
Avoid a reservation claim
Nothing has been reserved yet.
Clarify the location
Are you asking about availability at this branch?
Explain the next step
The next step is to check the requested variation.
Keep the record precise
Medium cotton, not medium linen.
Avoid premature certainty
I cannot confirm available or sold out before the check.
Invite correction
Please tell me if I have the size or material wrong.
Close the clarification
The item is confirmed; its stock status is still unknown.
6 language and precision notes
The blue shirt
A shared color may still leave more than one possible item.
In medium
In introduces the requested size rather than a different product name.
Cotton versus linen
The material distinction identifies separate advertised products in this case.
Yet
Not checked yet means unresolved, not unavailable.
At this branch
Limits the availability question to a specific store location.
Identified versus reserved
Identifying the correct item does not create a hold or confirm stock.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which read-back is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Blue cotton C218 in medium.

The correct read-back preserves the requested reference, material, color, and size.

2. Which stock statement is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. Local availability has not been checked yet.

No stock inquiry result exists yet, so both availability and reservation claims are unsupported.

3. Which next step follows the clarification?

Answer and explanation

B. Check the exact requested variation.

The exact item must be checked before discussing verified local availability.

4. Which distinction should a colleague receive?

Answer and explanation

A. C218 is cotton; L214 is linen.

The two references identify different products despite their shared color.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Which blue shirt?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Casey: Shopper / Hana: Retail associate

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • stock inquiry
  • product variation
  • substitution
  • size
  • cotton
  • reservation
  • advertised item
  • specification
  • item reference
  • local availability
  1. Casey
    Hi, do you have the blue shirt from this ad? The picture is small, so I can't make out the number.
  2. Hana
    There are two. I'll check the 1 first: did you want the cotton shirt or the linen one?
  3. Casey
    Cotton, please. It's the one on the left, with the sleeves rolled up. I hadn't noticed the other shirt.
  4. Hana
    The 2 one is C218. L214 is linen. What size would you like me to look for?
  5. Casey
    Medium. Small is usually too tight across my shoulders, and I don't want to take a chance on it.
  6. Hana
    I'll keep the 3 as medium. Same blue as the picture, or were you looking for another color?
  7. Casey
    This blue. I assumed that because it was in the ad, you'd have it here. Is that how it works?
  8. Hana
    An 4 isn't a live stock count. Let me check this branch before I tell you whether we have one.
  9. Casey
    Fair enough. There was a blue medium near the door, but the fabric felt different. Could that have been the linen shirt?
  10. Hana
    Possibly; we'd need its label. The 5 we're checking is C218, blue, medium, cotton.
  11. Casey
    Yes. I'm only asking about this shop for now. I can't get to another branch today.
  12. Hana
    Then I'll focus on 6. We can discuss other options later if you want to.
  13. Casey
    Does asking you to look put one on hold, or would I need to ask separately for that?
  14. Hana
    A 7 is separate. Nothing's been put aside; we're still checking what you want.
  15. Casey
    Could you read it back? Those two numbers are similar enough that I might have copied the wrong one.
  16. Hana
    Here's the 8: blue cotton C218 in medium. Not the linen L214.
  17. Casey
    That's right. If there's only linen, please let me know. I wouldn't want it swapped without checking with me.
  18. Hana
    Any 9 would be your choice. I'll check the shirt you asked for first.
  19. Casey
    Thanks. I'll stay nearby while you find out. I don't need you to look for a different size.
  20. Hana
    I'll start the 10 for blue cotton C218 in medium at this branch. We don't have the stock result yet.

Answers and explanations
  1. item reference Turn 2. The reference distinguishes the two advertised shirts before the associate searches for a particular size.
  2. cotton Turn 4. Cotton is the requested fiber, identifying C218 rather than linen L214.
  3. size Turn 6. The shopper requests medium; willingness to inquire does not authorize a different size.
  4. advertised item Turn 8. An advertisement identifies a promoted product but does not confirm this branch has the requested variation.
  5. product variation Turn 10. The variation combines product reference, material, color, and size; a shared color and size alone may misidentify it.
  6. local availability Turn 12. Local availability is stock of this exact item at this branch, not availability across the whole chain.
  7. reservation Turn 14. A reservation would be a confirmed hold; this conversation has not located or reserved an item.
  8. specification Turn 16. The complete specification is blue cotton C218 in medium; L214 refers to the other material.
  9. substitution Turn 18. A different material, size, or product changes the request and needs the shopper's agreement.
  10. stock inquiry Turn 20. The stock inquiry follows identification; no availability result or hold exists yet.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Casey: Hi, do you have the blue shirt from this ad? The picture is small, so I can't make out the number.
  2. Hana: There are two. I'll check the item reference first: did you want the cotton shirt or the linen one?
  3. Casey: Cotton, please. It's the one on the left, with the sleeves rolled up. I hadn't noticed the other shirt.
  4. Hana: The cotton one is C218. L214 is linen. What size would you like me to look for?
  5. Casey: Medium. Small is usually too tight across my shoulders, and I don't want to take a chance on it.
  6. Hana: I'll keep the size as medium. Same blue as the picture, or were you looking for another color?
  7. Casey: This blue. I assumed that because it was in the ad, you'd have it here. Is that how it works?
  8. Hana: An advertised item isn't a live stock count. Let me check this branch before I tell you whether we have one.
  9. Casey: Fair enough. There was a blue medium near the door, but the fabric felt different. Could that have been the linen shirt?
  10. Hana: Possibly; we'd need its label. The product variation we're checking is C218, blue, medium, cotton.
  11. Casey: Yes. I'm only asking about this shop for now. I can't get to another branch today.
  12. Hana: Then I'll focus on local availability. We can discuss other options later if you want to.
  13. Casey: Does asking you to look put one on hold, or would I need to ask separately for that?
  14. Hana: A reservation is separate. Nothing's been put aside; we're still checking what you want.
  15. Casey: Could you read it back? Those two numbers are similar enough that I might have copied the wrong one.
  16. Hana: Here's the specification: blue cotton C218 in medium. Not the linen L214.
  17. Casey: That's right. If there's only linen, please let me know. I wouldn't want it swapped without checking with me.
  18. Hana: Any substitution would be your choice. I'll check the shirt you asked for first.
  19. Casey: Thanks. I'll stay nearby while you find out. I don't need you to look for a different size.
  20. Hana: I'll start the stock inquiry for blue cotton C218 in medium at this branch. We don't have the stock result yet.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two blue shirts in one advertisement

An associate clarifies a shopper's request on the sales floor.

  1. Associate: There are two blue shirts in that advertisement. Do you mean the cotton or linen version?
  2. Shopper: The cotton one, please. I want a medium, but I can't find it here.
  3. Associate: The cotton code is C218. Let me confirm that against the item pictured on your phone.
  4. Shopper: Yes, that's the one. The linen shirt beside it looks almost the same color.
  5. Associate: I'll check the stock-keeping unit for medium C218, so we get that exact variation.
  6. Shopper: Does being in the advertisement mean you definitely have that size in this branch today?
  7. Associate: No. The advertisement identifies the product; local stock needs a separate check for your variation.
  8. Shopper: Thanks. Could I see a large for comparison if the medium isn't available here?
  9. Associate: We can discuss that after checking, but I won't substitute a different size without asking.
  10. Shopper: That's fine. Medium cotton C218 is my first choice; the other size is only a possibility.
The code is clearer than the display location

A shopper brings a loose item from a mixed display.

  1. Associate: You found this near the jackets, but its label identifies it as a lightweight overshirt.
  2. Shopper: I thought everything on that rail belonged to the same range and fitted similarly.
  3. Associate: Items can be moved. Let's check the product code and size label on this one.
  4. Shopper: The code is O316 and the label says small. Does it come in a medium?
  5. Associate: The listed size range includes medium; that doesn't yet confirm one is in this shop.
  6. Shopper: Could you check while I compare how the small feels in the fitting room?
  7. Associate: Certainly. The fitting rooms are beside the mirror, and I'll search this exact code.
  8. Shopper: Please don't bring a similar jacket instead. I need the same material and style.
  9. Associate: I'll look for medium O316, and tell you clearly if that variation isn't available.
  10. Shopper: Great. The label gives us a more precise starting point than where I found it.
A gift request without a guessed size

An associate helps a shopper buying a gift.

  1. Associate: What do you know about the item they liked: its color, material, or exact code?
  2. Shopper: Only that it was a green sweater in your window. I don't know their size.
  3. Associate: There are two green styles there. Could you point to the one they mentioned?
  4. Shopper: The one with the textured front. I don't want you to guess their size from a photograph.
  5. Associate: I won't. We can identify the style now and leave the size choice open.
  6. Shopper: Could I see the size range and the stated gift exchange terms before deciding?
  7. Associate: Yes. I'll show both, without treating an available range as proof every size is stocked.
  8. Shopper: That would help. I may ask them for a size before buying anything today.
  9. Associate: This is the textured style, code K420. You can use that code when you return.
  10. Shopper: I'll do that. Identifying the item first saves us choosing the wrong green sweater.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Which blue shirt?
  1. Read turns 2, 6, and 16 with C218 stressed. Your partner points to the cotton choice, not L214.
  2. Repeat the complete request using the four facts: C218, blue, medium, cotton.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Identify a different advertised item

An advertisement shows red wool W310 and red cotton C314 sweaters. The shopper wants red cotton C314 in large. Local stock is unchecked, and no item is reserved.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Associate: "The requested reference is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. C314

C314 identifies the cotton sweater the shopper selects, not the wool alternative.

2. Shopper: "The requested size is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. large

Large is the explicit size choice in this new scenario.

3. Associate: "Local stock remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unchecked

Identifying the product does not supply a verified availability result.

4. Shopper: "Nothing has been ___."

Answer and explanation

C. reserved

No hold has been created merely by clarifying the product request.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Finding the item the shopper actually means
Original fictional case: Shopper Casey asks associate Hana for the blue shirt in an advertisement. The advertisement contains two blue shirts: linen L214 and cotton C218. Casey wants the cotton version in medium, not the linen version or another size. Local availability has not been checked. Hana confirms C218, blue, medium, and cotton before beginning a stock inquiry. No item has been located, reserved, or sold, and the advertisement does not prove that the requested variation is available at this branch.
Language workshop: Which blue shirt?
Communication goal: Use a reference, material, and size to identify a product before making an unverified local-stock claim.
Language guidance: The exact request is blue cotton C218 in medium. Linen L214 is a different item even though it is also blue. Confirming the product does not establish stock, a reservation, or permission to substitute another variation.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The ad shows two blue shirts; do you mean cotton or linen? / Which size are you looking for? / The cotton shirt is C218. / L214 is the linen version. / Blue cotton C218 in medium; is that right? / That's the right shirt; let me check whether we have your size here. / The advertisement does not confirm stock at this branch. / I will use that exact reference and size for the stock inquiry. / I would ask before offering a different size or material. / Nothing has been reserved yet. / Are you asking about availability at this branch? / The next step is to check the requested variation. / Medium cotton, not medium linen. / I cannot confirm available or sold out before the check. / Please tell me if I have the size or material wrong. / The item is confirmed; its stock status is still unknown.
Vocabulary: item reference: Identifier used to distinguish a product. / stock-keeping unit: Internal identifier for a stocked product variation, often abbreviated SKU. / barcode: Machine-readable code used to identify an item or record. / product variation: Specific version differing in size, color, or another feature. / colorway: Color version offered within a product range. / size: Labelled dimension category such as medium. / cotton: Textile fiber identified for the requested shirt in this case. / linen: Different textile fiber used in the other advertised shirt. / fiber content: Information describing the fibers used in a textile. / advertised item: Product featured in a promotion or advertisement. / product image: Picture of a product, not proof of local stock. / range: Group of related products or variations. / local availability: Whether the exact variation is available at the relevant branch. / branch: Particular store location within a retailer. / shop floor: Customer-accessible area where merchandise is displayed. / stockroom: Storage area for merchandise not on the selling floor. / display sample: Item shown for viewing, not necessarily available for sale. / stock inquiry: Request to check whether a specific item is available. / stock record: System record of recorded merchandise quantities or status. / physical check: Verification of the actual item and its location. / reservation: Confirmed arrangement to hold an item under the store process. / substitution: Replacement with a different item or variation requiring agreement. / specification: Set of details defining the requested item. / planogram: Diagram assigning products and display space on a fixture.
Speaking task: Read turns 2, 6, and 16 with C218 stressed. Your partner points to the cotton choice, not L214. Repeat the complete request using the four facts: C218, blue, medium, cotton. Read turns 14 and 20. Keep locating stock and reserving it as separate steps.
Partner: Casey: Shopper / Hana: Retail associate
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An advertisement shows red wool W310 and red cotton C314 sweaters. The shopper wants red cotton C314 in large. Local stock is unchecked, and no item is reserved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Casey: That's right. If there's only linen, please let me know. I wouldn't want it swapped without checking with me.
Hana: Any substitution would be your choice. I'll check the shirt you asked for first.
Casey: Thanks. I'll stay nearby while you find out. I don't need you to look for a different size.
Hana: I'll start the stock inquiry for blue cotton C218 in medium at this branch. We don't have the stock result yet.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Communication goal: Use a reference, material, and size to identify a product before making an unverified local-stock claim.

Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The ad shows two blue shirts; do you mean cotton or linen? / Which size are you looking for? / The cotton shirt is C218. / L214 is the linen version. / Blue cotton C218 in medium; is that right? / That's the right shirt; let me check whether we have your size here. / The advertisement does not confirm stock at this branch. / I will use that exact reference and size for the stock inquiry. / I would ask before offering a different size or material. / Nothing has been reserved yet. / Are you asking about availability at this branch? / The next step is to check the requested variation. / Medium cotton, not medium linen. / I cannot confirm available or sold out before the check. / Please tell me if I have the size or material wrong. / The item is confirmed; its stock status is still unknown.
Vocabulary: item reference: Identifier used to distinguish a product. / stock-keeping unit: Internal identifier for a stocked product variation, often abbreviated SKU. / barcode: Machine-readable code used to identify an item or record. / product variation: Specific version differing in size, color, or another feature. / colorway: Color version offered within a product range. / size: Labelled dimension category such as medium. / cotton: Textile fiber identified for the requested shirt in this case. / linen: Different textile fiber used in the other advertised shirt. / fiber content: Information describing the fibers used in a textile. / advertised item: Product featured in a promotion or advertisement. / product image: Picture of a product, not proof of local stock. / range: Group of related products or variations. / local availability: Whether the exact variation is available at the relevant branch. / branch: Particular store location within a retailer. / shop floor: Customer-accessible area where merchandise is displayed. / stockroom: Storage area for merchandise not on the selling floor. / display sample: Item shown for viewing, not necessarily available for sale. / stock inquiry: Request to check whether a specific item is available. / stock record: System record of recorded merchandise quantities or status. / physical check: Verification of the actual item and its location. / reservation: Confirmed arrangement to hold an item under the store process. / substitution: Replacement with a different item or variation requiring agreement. / specification: Set of details defining the requested item. / planogram: Diagram assigning products and display space on a fixture.
Speaking task: Read turns 2, 6, and 16 with C218 stressed. Your partner points to the cotton choice, not L214. Repeat the complete request using the four facts: C218, blue, medium, cotton. Read turns 14 and 20. Keep locating stock and reserving it as separate steps.
Partner: Casey: Shopper / Hana: Retail associate
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An advertisement shows red wool W310 and red cotton C314 sweaters. The shopper wants red cotton C314 in large. Local stock is unchecked, and no item is reserved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Casey: That's right. If there's only linen, please let me know. I wouldn't want it swapped without checking with me.
Hana: Any substitution would be your choice. I'll check the shirt you asked for first.
Casey: Thanks. I'll stay nearby while you find out. I don't need you to look for a different size.
Hana: I'll start the stock inquiry for blue cotton C218 in medium at this branch. We don't have the stock result yet.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

02Comparing fit, materials, and label informationThe care label decides the comparison+

Your goal

Compare stated garment features and care requirements without inventing comfort, fit, or durability claims.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

The care label decides the comparison

Shopper Luis needs a machine-washable jacket and compares two $60 options with associate Priya. Jacket A is unlined and its label permits machine washing. Jacket B is lined and its label says dry-clean only. No other care details, durability tests, or fit measurements are supplied. A meets the stated washing requirement on the available label information; B does not. Luis asks to look at A next but has not tried it on or purchased it. Priya must not infer washing suitability from the presence or absence of a lining.

Luis: Shopper / Priya: Retail associate

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which jacket meets the stated machine-washing requirement?

Answer and explanation

D. A, according to its label

Jacket A's label permits machine washing, while B's says dry-clean only.

2. What do the two jackets cost?

Answer and explanation

C. $60 each

The brief prices each jacket at sixty dollars, not both together.

3. What is unverified?

Answer and explanation

A. Fit, comfort, and durability

No fitting, comfort evidence, or durability testing has been supplied.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The care label, not the lining, answers the washing question. A is unlined and permits machine washing; B is lined and says dry-clean only. Do not extend these two examples into a rule about all lined or unlined jackets.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
garment
Item of clothing.compare the garments
care label
Label stating how a garment should be cared for.read the care label
machine-washable
Suitable for machine washing under the actual label instructions.check the machine-washable claim
dry-clean only
Label instruction restricting cleaning to dry cleaning.explain the dry-clean-only instruction
hand wash
Care instruction specifying washing by hand.distinguish a hand-wash instruction
lining
Inner layer of a garment.identify the lining
lined
Made with an inner lining.describe the lined jacket
unlined
Made without a separate inner lining.describe the unlined jacket
outer fabric
Material forming the garment's external surface.identify the outer fabric
fiber blend
Combination of different fibers in a textile.read the fiber blend
woven fabric
Textile formed by interlacing sets of yarns.identify woven fabric
knit fabric
Textile formed through interlocking loops.distinguish knit fabric
seam
Line where pieces of fabric are joined.inspect the seam
hem
Finished edge of a garment or fabric section.identify the hem
cuff
Finished section at the end of a sleeve.compare cuff styles
fastening
Part used to close or secure the garment.check the fastening
zipper
Sliding fastening with interlocking elements.inspect the zipper
fit
How the garment sits on the individual wearer.check the fit
cut
Overall shaping or silhouette of a garment.compare the cut
size chart
Guide relating labelled sizes to stated measurements.consult the size chart
measurement
Quantified dimension used to compare fit.check the measurement
shrinkage
Reduction in dimensions, not ruled out by an unsupported claim.avoid a shrinkage guarantee
colorfastness
Ability of a textile to retain color under specified conditions.check colorfastness information
durability claim
Statement about resistance to wear that needs support.avoid an unsupported durability claim

Both prices are $60 each. Equal price does not imply equal care, fit, comfort, or durability. The shopper has asked to inspect A, not confirmed that it fits or completed a purchase. Use actual label details for any further care question.

C Notice the language

Use evidence instead of confident sales language

Words such as comfortable, durable, and easy-care can mean different things to different shoppers. Compare the label facts with the stated need, and distinguish a feature from a tested benefit. A useful recommendation can be conditional without sounding evasive.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask about the requirement
Is machine washing a requirement for you?
Read the first label
Jacket A's label permits machine washing.
Read the second label
Jacket B's label says dry-clean only.
Compare construction
A is unlined; B has a lining.
Avoid an inference
The lining alone does not tell us how to clean it.
State the equal prices
Both jackets are $60 each.
Connect fact and need
A's label permits machine washing, so let's look at that one first.
Keep fit separate
We still need to check how it fits you.
Avoid a comfort claim
Try it on and see how it feels; the lining alone won't tell us.
Avoid a durability promise
We do not have evidence here that one will last longer.
Offer inspection
Would you like to look at A more closely?
Use the full label
We should check the actual instructions for any further care detail.
Avoid invented settings
No washing temperature has been supplied in this comparison.
Clarify purchase status
Looking at A does not mean you have decided to buy it.
Invite a practical check
You can compare the fit before making your choice.
Summarize the evidence
Same price, different care instructions, fit still unverified.
6 language and precision notes
Each versus together
Sixty dollars each does not mean sixty dollars for both jackets.
Permits versus recommends
Use the actual wording supplied rather than strengthening or changing the instruction.
Only
Dry-clean only excludes treating the garment as machine-washable in this comparison.
Feature versus benefit
A lining is a construction feature, not automatic proof of comfort or durability.
Meets the requirement
This applies to the specified washing need, not every possible shopping preference.
Not tried on
Fit remains unverified until the relevant practical check occurs.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which recommendation uses the supplied evidence?

Answer and explanation

A. A meets your machine-washing requirement according to its label.

The recommendation connects the actual label to the shopper's specific requirement.

2. Which comparison is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

A. B will definitely last longer because it has a lining.

A lining alone does not establish comparative durability or wear performance.

3. Which statement preserves the purchase status?

Answer and explanation

B. Luis wants to inspect A but has not tried it on or bought it.

Inspection interest does not establish fit or a completed purchase.

4. What should resolve a further washing-temperature question?

Answer and explanation

D. The actual care-label instructions

The comparison supplies no temperature, so the actual garment information must be checked.

E Conversations

The care label decides the comparison

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Luis: Shopper / Priya: Retail associate

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • washing temperature
  • lining
  • only
  • care label
  • comfort claim
  • machine-washable
  • durability claim
  • fit
  • size chart
  • each
  1. Luis
    I need something I can put in the washing machine. These are both sixty dollars, aren't they? Which one would work?
  2. Priya
    Let us check the 1 on each. Jacket A permits machine washing, while jacket B says dry-clean only.
  3. Luis
    This one has a lining and that one doesn't. I usually check the fabric first, but perhaps that's not enough.
  4. Priya
    B has a 2, and A is unlined. Those are construction details, but the lining alone does not establish the cleaning requirements or their cause.
  5. Luis
    A sounds more suitable, then. I don't want regular dry-cleaning bills on top of the purchase price.
  6. Priya
    On the information here, A is 3 under its label instructions. That meets the specific care requirement you have stated.
  7. Luis
    Does dry-clean only really rule out machine washing, or is it just the maker's preferred method?
  8. Priya
    Yes. The word 4 is important in B's instruction. I should not describe that label as permission to machine wash the jacket.
  9. Luis
    And it's sixty for either one, not sixty for both? I want to be sure I'm comparing the same price.
  10. Priya
    Both are sixty dollars 5. The equal prices do not mean the care instructions or other characteristics are identical.
  11. Luis
    I'll look at A first. I haven't tried either jacket, so I still need to see how the shoulders fit.
  12. Priya
    Of course. The 6 is a separate question. Meeting your washing requirement does not tell us whether this particular jacket fits you as you want.
  13. Luis
    B feels different with that inner layer. Is that supposed to make it more comfortable, or does it depend on the wearer?
  14. Priya
    I cannot make a 7 from the lining alone. We can compare the stated features without promising how either will feel to you.
  15. Luis
    What about lasting longer? I can see the extra layer, but I don't know whether that tells us much about wear.
  16. Priya
    Correct. A 8 would need support. We have not checked evidence that one of these jackets will last longer than the other.
  17. Luis
    Let's look at A, then. I want to check it properly before I decide; I'm not ready to buy it yet.
  18. Priya
    I can help you with that. We can check the 9 and the actual garment information rather than treating the washing label as a complete recommendation.
  19. Luis
    Before I wash it, I'll need the full instructions. I can't read the temperature information from where I'm standing.
  20. Priya
    That is the right detail to verify. The 10 has not been confirmed in our discussion, and I will not invent one. For now, A meets the stated care requirement, with fit and purchase still open.

Answers and explanations
  1. care label Turn 2. Care label provides the relevant evidence about cleaning, rather than relying on equal price or visual similarity.
  2. lining Turn 4. Lining is a construction feature, not by itself proof of the garment's care requirements or the reason for them.
  3. machine-washable Turn 6. Machine-washable reflects A's supplied label instruction, subject to the actual care details rather than invented settings.
  4. only Turn 8. Only limits the stated method for B; it should not be paraphrased as optional dry cleaning or machine-washing permission.
  5. each Turn 10. Each applies the sixty-dollar price separately to either jacket, not to the pair together.
  6. fit Turn 12. Fit concerns how the jacket sits on Luis, which has not been established by its label or price.
  7. comfort claim Turn 14. Comfort claim would assert an experience not established by the supplied construction and care facts or by Luis trying the jacket.
  8. durability claim Turn 16. Durability claim needs evidence about wear; neither equal price nor the presence of a lining supplies that evidence here.
  9. size chart Turn 18. Size chart can provide stated measurements for a fitting comparison, without guaranteeing fit before the shopper assesses the actual garment.
  10. washing temperature Turn 20. Washing temperature has not been supplied in this comparison; the actual label must answer it rather than an invented general setting.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The care requirement decides the first comparison

An associate compares two jackets with a shopper.

  1. Associate: Both jackets cost sixty dollars. Is care, warmth, or fit your first priority today?
  2. Shopper: Care. I need something labelled for machine washing because I travel frequently for work.
  3. Associate: Jacket A's label permits that. Jacket B is lined and marked dry-clean only.
  4. Shopper: Then the similar price doesn't mean they have the same care requirements at all.
  5. Associate: Correct. The labels give different instructions, even though both jackets share the same displayed price.
  6. Shopper: I'll consider A first. Does being unlined automatically mean it will fit more loosely?
  7. Associate: No. Lining and fit are separate features; trying it on will help you compare.
  8. Shopper: May I try the medium and large rather than choose from the label alone?
  9. Associate: Certainly. I'll bring both available sizes so you can compare the shoulder and body fit.
  10. Shopper: That's useful. The care label narrows the choice, but I still need the right fit.
Sleeve length and body fit are different

An associate checks a shopper's concern outside the fitting room.

  1. Associate: Which part doesn't feel right: the body, the shoulders, or the length of the sleeves?
  2. Shopper: The body feels fine, but the sleeves cover more of my hands than I expected.
  3. Associate: So going up a size may not address the issue you're describing with sleeve length.
  4. Shopper: Exactly. I don't want more room everywhere just because the sleeve shape isn't suitable.
  5. Associate: We have another cut in the same listed size. Would you like to compare that?
  6. Shopper: Yes, as long as it's clear we're comparing a different cut, not identical sizing across styles.
  7. Associate: Certainly. Size labels don't guarantee the same fit in every design or product range.
  8. Shopper: Could you show me where the shoulder seam sits on each one for comparison?
  9. Associate: Yes, here is the stitched join on each garment. Tell me which feels more comfortable.
  10. Shopper: The second style suits me better. I'll check its care label before making my decision.
Reading a label without inventing a guarantee

A shopper asks about an unfamiliar fabric-care symbol.

  1. Shopper: Does this label mean the sweater will never shrink, whatever washing method I use?
  2. Associate: No. The label gives care information; it doesn't make an unlimited guarantee about every method.
  3. Shopper: Could you help me find the manufacturer's written explanation rather than guess at the symbol?
  4. Associate: Yes. This item has a printed care explanation on the reverse of the label.
  5. Shopper: I see it now. The instructions are more specific than what I assumed from the picture.
  6. Associate: Would you like to compare another item with care requirements that better fit your routine?
  7. Shopper: Please. I'd prefer a clearly labelled option I can maintain without changing my usual arrangements.
  8. Associate: This alternative has different stated care information. Let's compare the labels before discussing its fit.
  9. Shopper: That helps. I won't treat your explanation as permission to ignore the manufacturer's instructions.
  10. Associate: Exactly. We can clarify the wording while keeping the actual care directions with the product.

Lesson 02: Comparing fit, materials, and label information
Original fictional case: Shopper Luis needs a machine-washable jacket and compares two $60 options with associate Priya. Jacket A is unlined and its label permits machine washing. Jacket B is lined and its label says dry-clean only. No other care details, durability tests, or fit measurements are supplied. A meets the stated washing requirement on the available label information; B does not. Luis asks to look at A next but has not tried it on or purchased it. Priya must not infer washing suitability from the presence or absence of a lining.
Language workshop: The care label decides the comparison
Communication goal: Compare stated garment features and care requirements without inventing comfort, fit, or durability claims.
Language guidance: The care label, not the lining, answers the washing question. A is unlined and permits machine washing; B is lined and says dry-clean only. Do not extend these two examples into a rule about all lined or unlined jackets.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Is machine washing a requirement for you? / Jacket A's label permits machine washing. / Jacket B's label says dry-clean only. / A is unlined; B has a lining. / The lining alone does not tell us how to clean it. / Both jackets are $60 each. / A's label permits machine washing, so let's look at that one first. / We still need to check how it fits you. / Try it on and see how it feels; the lining alone won't tell us. / We do not have evidence here that one will last longer. / Would you like to look at A more closely? / We should check the actual instructions for any further care detail. / No washing temperature has been supplied in this comparison. / Looking at A does not mean you have decided to buy it. / You can compare the fit before making your choice. / Same price, different care instructions, fit still unverified.
Vocabulary: garment: Item of clothing. / care label: Label stating how a garment should be cared for. / machine-washable: Suitable for machine washing under the actual label instructions. / dry-clean only: Label instruction restricting cleaning to dry cleaning. / hand wash: Care instruction specifying washing by hand. / lining: Inner layer of a garment. / lined: Made with an inner lining. / unlined: Made without a separate inner lining. / outer fabric: Material forming the garment's external surface. / fiber blend: Combination of different fibers in a textile. / woven fabric: Textile formed by interlacing sets of yarns. / knit fabric: Textile formed through interlocking loops. / seam: Line where pieces of fabric are joined. / hem: Finished edge of a garment or fabric section. / cuff: Finished section at the end of a sleeve. / fastening: Part used to close or secure the garment. / zipper: Sliding fastening with interlocking elements. / fit: How the garment sits on the individual wearer. / cut: Overall shaping or silhouette of a garment. / size chart: Guide relating labelled sizes to stated measurements. / measurement: Quantified dimension used to compare fit. / shrinkage: Reduction in dimensions, not ruled out by an unsupported claim. / colorfastness: Ability of a textile to retain color under specified conditions. / durability claim: Statement about resistance to wear that needs support.
Speaking task: Read A's and B's care instructions aloud; emphasize only in B's instruction. Read the sixty-dollars-each line, then the fit question, without claiming a purchase. Use turns 4 and 20 to separate a lining from the actual care instructions.
Partner: Luis: Shopper / Priya: Retail associate
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Jacket C is lined and permits machine washing. Jacket D is unlined and says dry-clean only. Both are $70 each. The shopper requires machine washing and has tried neither on.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Luis: Let's look at A, then. I want to check it properly before I decide; I'm not ready to buy it yet.
Priya: I can help you with that. We can check the size chart and the actual garment information rather than treating the washing label as a complete recommendation.
Luis: Before I wash it, I'll need the full instructions. I can't read the temperature information from where I'm standing.
Priya: That is the right detail to verify. The washing temperature has not been confirmed in our discussion, and I will not invent one. For now, A meets the stated care requirement, with fit and purchase still open.

03Resolving shelf prices and offer conditionsOne bottle is not half the bundle+

Your goal

Explain a quantity condition, distinguish average and single-item prices, and respect the shopper choice.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

One bottle is not half the bundle

Shopper Nia has one juice bottle with a regular single-bottle price of $3. The shelf offer is two qualifying bottles for $5; single bottles remain $3. Nia expects one bottle to cost $2.50. Associate Ben explains that $2.50 is the average per bottle only when buying the qualifying pair. Two at regular prices would cost $6, so the pair saves $1 against that comparison, but it requires $2 more total spending than one $3 bottle. Nia chooses one bottle at $3. No other charges apply in the exercise.

Nia: Shopper / Ben: Retail associate

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the price of one bottle?

Answer and explanation

B. $3

The offer leaves the single-bottle price at three dollars.

2. What qualifies for the $5 offer?

Answer and explanation

D. Two qualifying bottles

The stated promotion requires a qualifying pair, not a single item.

3. Which choice does Nia make?

Answer and explanation

A. One bottle at $3

Nia chooses the single bottle after the complete comparison is explained.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Two bottles for $5 works out to $2.50 per bottle only within that qualifying purchase. It does not change the stated $3 single-bottle price. One bottle and two bottles are different purchase quantities with different complete totals.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
shelf label
Displayed product and price information at the shelf.check the shelf label
multibuy offer
Promotion requiring purchase of a specified number of items.explain the multibuy offer
qualifying item
Product that meets the offer's stated conditions.identify a qualifying item
quantity condition
Required number of items for an offer.state the quantity condition
single-item price
Price charged when buying one item alone.confirm the single-item price
regular price
Non-promotional price used as the stated comparison.compare regular prices
bundle total
Complete price for the grouped qualifying items.state the bundle total
average price
Total divided by the number of items, not necessarily the one-item selling price.explain the average price
price per item
Amount per individual item under the specified purchase conditions.compare the price per item
unit pricing
Price stated per standard quantity such as a liter or kilogram.distinguish unit pricing
comparison basis
Quantity and conditions used to calculate a saving.identify the comparison basis
saving
Reduction against the specified comparison amount.calculate the saving
extra spending
Increase in total outlay compared with another purchase choice.explain the extra spending
minimum purchase
Smallest quantity or spend needed to qualify.clarify the minimum purchase
mix-and-match
Offer allowing specified different items to form a qualifying group.check mix-and-match eligibility
exclusion
Item or condition not covered by an offer.explain an exclusion
promotion period
Dates during which an offer applies.check the promotion period
loyalty price
Price available under a stated membership condition.clarify the loyalty price
coupon
Voucher or code offering a benefit under specified terms.check a coupon
redemption condition
Requirement for using a coupon or offer.explain redemption conditions
price query
Shopper question about the amount or conditions charged.resolve a price query
tare
Weight of packaging excluded when finding a product's net weight.deduct the correct tare
basket total
Total for the shopper's selected goods.confirm the basket total
purchase preference
Shopper's choice among clearly explained options.respect the purchase preference

The pair saves $1 compared with two bottles at $3 each. It still costs $2 more than buying only one $3 bottle. Always name the comparison basis before saying cheaper or saves money; do not imply a larger basket necessarily lowers total spending.

C Notice the language

Explain the offer without pushing the extra purchase

A lower average price does not necessarily serve a shopper who needs only one item. State the quantity condition and both complete totals neutrally. Distinguish saving against two regular-price items from spending less than the single item already chosen.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the calculation
I see how you arrived at $2.50 by dividing the pair price.
State the condition
The $5 offer applies to two qualifying bottles.
Confirm the single price
One bottle on its own remains $3.
Explain the average
$2.50 is the average per bottle when you buy the pair.
Give both complete totals
One is $3; two qualifying bottles are $5.
Name the comparison
Two at the regular price would cost $6.
Calculate the saving
The pair saves $1 compared with that $6 total.
Explain the extra outlay
It is still $2 more spending than buying one bottle.
Avoid a false single price
The bundle average is not the one-bottle selling price.
Keep qualification explicit
We should check that both bottles qualify for the offer.
Avoid adding conditions
No membership condition is stated in this offer.
Respect the need
You do not have to buy a second bottle to keep the single-item option.
Ask for the choice
Would you prefer one at $3 or two at $5?
Confirm the selection
You have chosen one bottle at $3.
Read back quantity
The selected quantity is one, not the pair.
Close the query
The offer is clear, and your single-bottle choice remains unchanged.
6 language and precision notes
For versus each
Two for $5 gives a group price; $3 each describes the regular single price here.
Average versus sale price
Division explains the pair's average but does not create a one-item offer.
Compared with
This phrase names the baseline for a saving claim.
Qualifying
The offer applies only to products meeting the stated terms.
More spending versus better value
A lower per-item price can still require a higher total payment.
Choice versus pressure
Explain both options without treating the larger purchase as the only sensible one.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. $2.50 is the pair's average per bottle; one alone is $3.

The promotion has a quantity condition that division alone does not remove.

2. What is the saving on the pair against two regular-price bottles?

Answer and explanation

A. $1

Two regular-price bottles cost six dollars, so the five-dollar pair saves one.

3. How much more does the pair cost than buying one bottle?

Answer and explanation

D. $2

The five-dollar total exceeds the three-dollar single purchase by two dollars.

4. Which response respects the final choice?

Answer and explanation

D. One bottle at $3; I will keep that quantity.

The shopper explicitly selects one, and its stated single price remains three dollars.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

One bottle is not half the bundle

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nia: Shopper / Ben: Retail associate

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • quantity condition
  • price query
  • quantity read-back
  • single-item price
  • qualifying items
  • average price
  • extra spending
  • purchase preference
  • basket total
  • comparison basis
  1. Nia
    This bottle is coming up at three dollars, but the shelf says two for five. Shouldn't one be two fifty?
  2. Ben
    I see why you'd read it that way. There's a 1: the five-dollar price applies when you buy two.
  3. Nia
    I divided five by two. So that figure isn't wrong; it just doesn't apply to the one bottle I've brought?
  4. Ben
    Right. Two fifty is the 2 within that pair. One on its own is three dollars.
  5. Nia
    That's the part I missed. I thought the shelf label had replaced the old price for every bottle.
  6. Ben
    Here the 3 stays three dollars. I'll show you both totals so you can choose.
  7. Nia
    If I did get another, could it be any juice, or would I need to look for the same offer?
  8. Ben
    Both must be 4. We'd check the shelf offer for the exact products included.
  9. Nia
    What's the actual saving on two? I'm trying to work out whether it's worth carrying an extra bottle home.
  10. Ben
    The 5 is six dollars for two at the regular price. The pair is five, saving one dollar.
  11. Nia
    But compared with just paying for this one, I'd still be handing over more money today.
  12. Ben
    Yes, two dollars of 6: five instead of three. The lower per-bottle average doesn't lower today's total for one.
  13. Nia
    I'll stick with one. I only need it for lunch, and I don't have room in my bag for another.
  14. Ben
    No problem. I'll keep your 7 as one bottle. The offer is there if you need two.
  15. Nia
    Thank you. Please don't add another one. I was asking about the price, not asking for the pair.
  16. Ben
    My 8 is one bottle at three dollars. That's all we're keeping in this basket.
  17. Nia
    Do I need a loyalty card for the three-dollar price, or was the whole difference just the number of bottles?
  18. Ben
    No membership condition is stated here. This 9 is about one bottle versus the qualifying pair.
  19. Nia
    All right. One bottle, three dollars. I understand the shelf sign now; I'll check the quantity wording next time.
  20. Ben
    Your 10 is three dollars, with no other charges in this case. The quantity stays one.

Answers and explanations
  1. quantity condition Turn 2. The five-dollar promotion requires two qualifying bottles; it does not change the single-bottle price.
  2. average price Turn 4. Five dollars divided by two is the average within the pair, not a separate one-bottle offer.
  3. single-item price Turn 6. The stated price for one bottle alone remains three dollars.
  4. qualifying items Turn 8. Each bottle must meet the stated offer terms; another product cannot automatically complete the pair.
  5. comparison basis Turn 10. Two bottles at the regular three-dollar price total six dollars; the pair offer saves one against that amount.
  6. extra spending Turn 12. Five dollars for the pair is two dollars more total spending than three dollars for one.
  7. purchase preference Turn 14. Nia chooses the single bottle based on need and total spending; the offer does not override that choice.
  8. quantity read-back Turn 16. The read-back confirms one bottle and prevents an explanation of the offer becoming an unintended purchase.
  9. price query Turn 18. The question concerns the quantity requirement, not a loyalty condition that the offer does not state.
  10. basket total Turn 20. One bottle at three dollars totals three dollars; this fictional case has no other charges.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Nia: This bottle is coming up at three dollars, but the shelf says two for five. Shouldn't one be two fifty?
  2. Ben: I see why you'd read it that way. There's a quantity condition: the five-dollar price applies when you buy two.
  3. Nia: I divided five by two. So that figure isn't wrong; it just doesn't apply to the one bottle I've brought?
  4. Ben: Right. Two fifty is the average price within that pair. One on its own is three dollars.
  5. Nia: That's the part I missed. I thought the shelf label had replaced the old price for every bottle.
  6. Ben: Here the single-item price stays three dollars. I'll show you both totals so you can choose.
  7. Nia: If I did get another, could it be any juice, or would I need to look for the same offer?
  8. Ben: Both must be qualifying items. We'd check the shelf offer for the exact products included.
  9. Nia: What's the actual saving on two? I'm trying to work out whether it's worth carrying an extra bottle home.
  10. Ben: The comparison basis is six dollars for two at the regular price. The pair is five, saving one dollar.
  11. Nia: But compared with just paying for this one, I'd still be handing over more money today.
  12. Ben: Yes, two dollars of extra spending: five instead of three. The lower per-bottle average doesn't lower today's total for one.
  13. Nia: I'll stick with one. I only need it for lunch, and I don't have room in my bag for another.
  14. Ben: No problem. I'll keep your purchase preference as one bottle. The offer is there if you need two.
  15. Nia: Thank you. Please don't add another one. I was asking about the price, not asking for the pair.
  16. Ben: My quantity read-back is one bottle at three dollars. That's all we're keeping in this basket.
  17. Nia: Do I need a loyalty card for the three-dollar price, or was the whole difference just the number of bottles?
  18. Ben: No membership condition is stated here. This price query is about one bottle versus the qualifying pair.
  19. Nia: All right. One bottle, three dollars. I understand the shelf sign now; I'll check the quantity wording next time.
  20. Ben: Your basket total is three dollars, with no other charges in this case. The quantity stays one.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

One bottle is not half the bundle offer

A cashier explains the fictional juice promotion.

  1. Cashier: You have one qualifying bottle. Its single price is three dollars under this offer.
  2. Shopper: The shelf says two for five, so I expected this one to be two-fifty.
  3. Cashier: This multibuy offer requires two qualifying bottles. Then each would average two-fifty.
  4. Shopper: I only need one. Are you saying the displayed deal doesn't change its individual price?
  5. Cashier: That's right for these stated terms. One remains three; two qualifying bottles total five.
  6. Shopper: Could I add any flavor, or does the second bottle need the same offer label?
  7. Cashier: Both must qualify. We can check the shelf label for the flavor you're considering.
  8. Shopper: Then I'll keep just this one today. I don't want an extra bottle I won't use.
  9. Cashier: Certainly. I'll leave the quantity at one and confirm the three-dollar price before payment.
  10. Shopper: Three dollars for this one is fine. Please leave the extra bottle off my purchase.
The label belongs to the smaller pack

An associate checks a price complaint at a shelf.

  1. Associate: Let's match the shelf-edge label to your pack before deciding which price applies.
  2. Shopper: I picked it from this space, so I assumed the label underneath referred to it.
  3. Associate: The label describes a six-pack, while your box contains twelve and has a different code.
  4. Shopper: That explains the mismatch. Can you find the displayed price for this actual twelve-pack?
  5. Associate: Yes. I'll check the matching code rather than assume the six-pack price applies.
  6. Shopper: Please don't replace my box with six just to match the lower label I mentioned.
  7. Associate: I won't. You're asking for a price check on the twelve-pack, not a substitution.
  8. Shopper: Exactly. Once I know the correct total, I can choose which quantity I want.
  9. Associate: The matching twelve-pack label says nine dollars. Would you like to keep it or compare the six?
  10. Shopper: I'll keep twelve at nine. Thank you for identifying the mismatch before I reached checkout.
A larger pack is not automatically the cheaper choice

A shopper compares two fictional quantities of detergent.

  1. Associate: The one-liter bottle is four dollars; the two-liter bottle is seven dollars today.
  2. Shopper: So the bigger one costs more overall but less for each liter, correct?
  3. Associate: Yes. The smaller is four per liter, while the larger works out at three-fifty per liter.
  4. Shopper: I only have five dollars to spend. The better unit price doesn't fit that limit.
  5. Associate: Then the smaller bottle meets your total budget, although its price per liter is higher.
  6. Shopper: I appreciate that distinction. I don't want to be pushed toward a larger purchase just for a ratio.
  7. Associate: Of course. Unit price is one comparison; the amount you need and can spend also matter.
  8. Shopper: I'll take one liter. Does that displayed four dollars depend on buying anything else?
  9. Associate: No, the supplied price is for one bottle without an additional purchase condition.
  10. Shopper: That's the one I want. Please keep the comparison tied to today's displayed prices.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

One bottle is not half the bundle
  1. Read the two totals as options: one for $3, two qualifying bottles for $5.
  2. Repeat the two comparisons: $1 saved against two regular bottles; $2 more than one.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Explain a different quantity offer

One qualifying soup can is $4. The offer is three qualifying cans for $10, with no other charges. The shopper selects one can. Three at regular prices would cost $12.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Associate: "The offer requires ___ qualifying cans."

Answer and explanation

D. three

Three is the stated quantity required for the ten-dollar promotion.

2. Shopper: "My one-can choice costs ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. four

The single-can price remains four dollars despite the bundle offer.

3. Associate: "The bundle saves ___ dollars against three regular-price cans."

Answer and explanation

C. two

Twelve dollars minus the ten-dollar bundle total gives a two-dollar saving.

4. Shopper: "My selected quantity remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. one

The shopper chooses one rather than accepting the larger bundle.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Resolving shelf prices and offer conditions
Original fictional case: Shopper Nia has one juice bottle with a regular single-bottle price of $3. The shelf offer is two qualifying bottles for $5; single bottles remain $3. Nia expects one bottle to cost $2.50. Associate Ben explains that $2.50 is the average per bottle only when buying the qualifying pair. Two at regular prices would cost $6, so the pair saves $1 against that comparison, but it requires $2 more total spending than one $3 bottle. Nia chooses one bottle at $3. No other charges apply in the exercise.
Language workshop: One bottle is not half the bundle
Communication goal: Explain a quantity condition, distinguish average and single-item prices, and respect the shopper choice.
Language guidance: Two bottles for $5 works out to $2.50 per bottle only within that qualifying purchase. It does not change the stated $3 single-bottle price. One bottle and two bottles are different purchase quantities with different complete totals.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I see how you arrived at $2.50 by dividing the pair price. / The $5 offer applies to two qualifying bottles. / One bottle on its own remains $3. / $2.50 is the average per bottle when you buy the pair. / One is $3; two qualifying bottles are $5. / Two at the regular price would cost $6. / The pair saves $1 compared with that $6 total. / It is still $2 more spending than buying one bottle. / The bundle average is not the one-bottle selling price. / We should check that both bottles qualify for the offer. / No membership condition is stated in this offer. / You do not have to buy a second bottle to keep the single-item option. / Would you prefer one at $3 or two at $5? / You have chosen one bottle at $3. / The selected quantity is one, not the pair. / The offer is clear, and your single-bottle choice remains unchanged.
Vocabulary: shelf label: Displayed product and price information at the shelf. / multibuy offer: Promotion requiring purchase of a specified number of items. / qualifying item: Product that meets the offer's stated conditions. / quantity condition: Required number of items for an offer. / single-item price: Price charged when buying one item alone. / regular price: Non-promotional price used as the stated comparison. / bundle total: Complete price for the grouped qualifying items. / average price: Total divided by the number of items, not necessarily the one-item selling price. / price per item: Amount per individual item under the specified purchase conditions. / unit pricing: Price stated per standard quantity such as a liter or kilogram. / comparison basis: Quantity and conditions used to calculate a saving. / saving: Reduction against the specified comparison amount. / extra spending: Increase in total outlay compared with another purchase choice. / minimum purchase: Smallest quantity or spend needed to qualify. / mix-and-match: Offer allowing specified different items to form a qualifying group. / exclusion: Item or condition not covered by an offer. / promotion period: Dates during which an offer applies. / loyalty price: Price available under a stated membership condition. / coupon: Voucher or code offering a benefit under specified terms. / redemption condition: Requirement for using a coupon or offer. / price query: Shopper question about the amount or conditions charged. / tare: Weight of packaging excluded when finding a product's net weight. / basket total: Total for the shopper's selected goods. / purchase preference: Shopper's choice among clearly explained options.
Speaking task: Read the two totals as options: one for $3, two qualifying bottles for $5. Repeat the two comparisons: $1 saved against two regular bottles; $2 more than one. Read the closing lines without changing the shopper's one-bottle choice.
Partner: Nia: Shopper / Ben: Retail associate
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: One qualifying soup can is $4. The offer is three qualifying cans for $10, with no other charges. The shopper selects one can. Three at regular prices would cost $12.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Nia: Do I need a loyalty card for the three-dollar price, or was the whole difference just the number of bottles?
Ben: No membership condition is stated here. This price query is about one bottle versus the qualifying pair.
Nia: All right. One bottle, three dollars. I understand the shelf sign now; I'll check the quantity wording next time.
Ben: Your basket total is three dollars, with no other charges in this case. The quantity stays one.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
