07Managing queues and offering practical assistanceA checkout route that works+

Your goal

Ask what assistance is preferred, offer an available checkout route, and respect individual choices and position in line.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

A checkout route that works

Shopper Sam uses a mobility aid and says the current checkout is too narrow. Associate Lina knows that accessible checkout lane four is open, reached by the clear main aisle to the left. Sam prefers spoken directions, keeps personal belongings and the mobility aid under their own control, and accepts the move to lane four. Lina will ask colleague Noor to preserve the current place in line during the move; that request is not yet confirmed. No diagnosis, physical assistance, or handling of belongings is requested.

Sam: Shopper / Lina: Retail associate

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What problem does Sam identify?

Answer and explanation

B. The current checkout is too narrow

Sam identifies the width of the current checkout, not a store-wide finding.

2. Which available route is offered?

Answer and explanation

B. Main aisle to the left toward open lane four

The brief supplies an open accessible checkout and a clear main-aisle route.

3. What help does Sam prefer?

Answer and explanation

C. Spoken directions, with belongings and aid left under personal control

The shopper chooses directions and does not authorize handling personal belongings or the aid.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Lane four is open and reached by the supplied main-aisle route. Sam chooses spoken directions and retains control of belongings and the mobility aid. Lina will ask Noor to preserve the place in line, but that coordination is not yet confirmed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
queue
Line of shoppers waiting for service.manage the queue
place in line
Shopper's position in the service order.preserve a place in line
checkout lane
Specific path and counter used for payment.identify the checkout lane
accessible checkout
Checkout identified as accessible in the supplied store scenario.offer the accessible checkout
main aisle
Principal route through the selling area.describe the main aisle
clear route
Route free of the stated obstruction in the scenario.identify a clear route
narrow passage
Space the shopper reports as too restricted for access.acknowledge a narrow passage
mobility aid
Device a person uses to support movement.respect the mobility aid
walker
Walking aid providing support through a frame.identify a walker when relevant
wheelchair
Mobility device used for seated movement.respect wheelchair users' choices
personal belongings
Items owned or carried by the shopper.ask before handling belongings
preferred assistance
Help the shopper says would be useful.ask about preferred assistance
spoken directions
Verbal explanation of where to go.offer spoken directions
physical guidance
Hands-on assistance, not authorized by a general offer to help.ask before physical guidance
permission
Agreement to a specific action.obtain permission before handling items
independent movement
Shopper moving under their own control.respect independent movement
direct address
Speaking to the shopper rather than only a companion.use direct address
companion
Person accompanying the shopper, not automatically their spokesperson.avoid addressing only the companion
route confirmation
Check that the proposed path is understood and suitable for the request.give a route confirmation
colleague coordination
Communication to arrange the next service step with another worker.coordinate with a colleague
open lane
Checkout currently available for use.identify the open lane
queue transfer
Move between lines while managing the service order.arrange a queue transfer
assistance boundary
Limit on what help the shopper has authorized.respect the assistance boundary
pending request
Asked-for action not yet confirmed or completed.distinguish a pending request

Do not turn a helpful offer into unrequested touching or a medical inquiry. Ask about the practical assistance needed for this route. The fictional description identifies an available checkout; it does not certify real-world accessibility or replace applicable requirements.

C Notice the language

Ask rather than take over

Address the shopper directly and ask what help would be useful. A mobility aid is not an invitation to touch it or a reason to make assumptions about the person. Give practical information and respect the requested form of assistance without demanding a personal explanation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the barrier
I understand that this checkout is too narrow for you.
Ask about preferred help
What help would you prefer?
Offer the available lane
The accessible checkout at lane four is open.
Describe the route
The clear main aisle to the left leads to lane four.
Check the preference
Would spoken directions be helpful?
Respect belongings
I will not handle your belongings without your permission.
Respect the mobility aid
I will leave your mobility aid under your control.
Avoid a personal inquiry
We can focus on the checkout route and the help you want.
Address the shopper
Let me check the next step with you directly.
Preserve service order
I will ask Noor to preserve your place during the move.
Keep coordination accurate
That request still needs confirmation.
Avoid an unverified promise
I have not yet confirmed the queue arrangement with Noor.
Confirm the accepted help
You prefer directions, without physical assistance.
Invite a route question
Is there any part of the route you would like clarified?
Keep the choice with the shopper
Please tell me if you prefer a different form of help.
Close the arrangement
Lane four is available; I will coordinate the place-in-line request.
6 language and precision notes
Would you like
Offers a choice without assuming that assistance is needed or wanted.
Directly
Speaking to the shopper preserves agency even when a companion is present.
Permission scope
Accepting directions does not authorize touching a bag or mobility aid.
Too narrow
This reports the shopper's practical barrier without requiring a diagnosis.
Will ask versus confirmed
A promised coordination step is not proof that the colleague has accepted it.
Preserve versus restart
Preserving the place means addressing continuity rather than automatically sending the shopper to the back.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening respects the shopper's choice?

Answer and explanation

C. What help would you prefer with the route to lane four?

The question asks about practical preferences without assuming permission or demanding personal medical details.

2. Which action is authorized?

Answer and explanation

A. Giving spoken directions

Sam accepts directions and retains control of belongings and the mobility aid.

3. Which statement accurately describes the queue arrangement?

Answer and explanation

B. Lina will ask Noor to preserve the place; confirmation is pending.

The brief distinguishes the planned colleague request from an accepted arrangement.

4. Which route information is supplied?

Answer and explanation

C. Clear main aisle to the left, toward open lane four

The scenario provides a specific usable route, not a certification or invented shortcut.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

A checkout route that works

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sam: Shopper / Lina: Retail associate

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • colleague coordination
  • assistance boundary
  • pending request
  • main aisle
  • place in line
  • accessible checkout
  • permission
  • spoken directions
  • route confirmation
  • direct address
  1. Sam
    This lane's too narrow for my mobility aid. Is there a wider checkout I can use?
  2. Lina
    Yes. The 1 at lane four is open. Before I offer help, what would you prefer?
  3. Sam
    Just tell me the way, please. I'll move myself and keep my bag with me.
  4. Lina
    I will give 2 and leave your belongings and aid under your control. I will not assume you want me to carry or move anything.
  5. Sam
    Which way is lane four? I can't see the number beyond the people in front.
  6. Lina
    The 3 to your left leads toward lane four. That is the clear route available, rather than trying to continue through this narrow checkout.
  7. Sam
    That route sounds fine. I've been waiting in this line for a while, though. Will I lose my place?
  8. Lina
    I will ask Noor to preserve your 4 during the move. I do not want to treat the change of lane as a reason to ignore the time you have already waited.
  9. Sam
    Has Noor already agreed, or do we need to wait for an answer before I move across?
  10. Lina
    The 5 still needs confirmation. I will ask Noor, but I should not tell you the arrangement has already been accepted.
  11. Sam
    Please ask. Directions are enough for the move; I don't need anyone to take hold of the aid.
  12. Lina
    Understood. Your 6 is clear. Directions are welcome; touching the mobility aid or taking your bag has not been authorized.
  13. Sam
    Thanks for checking with me. It's awkward when someone takes my bag before I can explain what would help.
  14. Lina
    I will seek 7 before any different kind of help. Your answer about directions should not be treated as agreement to every possible action.
  15. Sam
    I'm happy to use lane four once you've checked the queue arrangement. Could you repeat the direction?
  16. Lina
    Let me give a 8: use the clear main aisle to the left, toward the open lane four. I will also follow up with Noor about the queue.
  17. Sam
    Left, then lane four. You've explained it clearly. You can keep talking to me rather than my companion.
  18. Lina
    Of course. I will use 9 and keep discussing the arrangement with you. The practical route and your preference are what we need to clarify.
  19. Sam
    Yes, that's what I need: directions, my belongings left with me, and help keeping my place.
  20. Lina
    That is accurate. The 10 is the queue arrangement with Noor. Lane four is open, your preferred assistance is clear, and I will not describe the coordination as complete until it is confirmed.

Answers and explanations
  1. accessible checkout Turn 2. Accessible checkout is the available lane four identified in this scenario, offering a practical alternative to the reported narrow lane.
  2. spoken directions Turn 4. Spoken directions are the form of assistance Sam chooses, without authorizing physical guidance or handling personal items.
  3. main aisle Turn 6. Main aisle is the clear route to the left supplied in the scenario, leading toward open lane four.
  4. place in line Turn 8. Place in line is Sam's existing service position, which Lina will ask the colleague to preserve during the move.
  5. colleague coordination Turn 10. Colleague coordination is still pending; Lina has proposed asking Noor but has not received confirmation.
  6. assistance boundary Turn 12. Assistance boundary limits help to the requested directions and coordination, excluding unrequested handling of the mobility aid or belongings.
  7. permission Turn 14. Permission must apply to a specific proposed action; a general offer of help does not authorize touching or carrying.
  8. route confirmation Turn 16. Route confirmation repeats the left-hand main aisle and lane-four destination so the shopper can verify the proposed path.
  9. direct address Turn 18. Direct address means speaking to Sam about Sam's preferences, rather than treating a companion as the automatic decision-maker.
  10. pending request Turn 20. Pending request is Noor's place-in-line coordination, which remains to be confirmed rather than reported as already complete.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sam: This lane's too narrow for my mobility aid. Is there a wider checkout I can use?
  2. Lina: Yes. The accessible checkout at lane four is open. Before I offer help, what would you prefer?
  3. Sam: Just tell me the way, please. I'll move myself and keep my bag with me.
  4. Lina: I will give spoken directions and leave your belongings and aid under your control. I will not assume you want me to carry or move anything.
  5. Sam: Which way is lane four? I can't see the number beyond the people in front.
  6. Lina: The main aisle to your left leads toward lane four. That is the clear route available, rather than trying to continue through this narrow checkout.
  7. Sam: That route sounds fine. I've been waiting in this line for a while, though. Will I lose my place?
  8. Lina: I will ask Noor to preserve your place in line during the move. I do not want to treat the change of lane as a reason to ignore the time you have already waited.
  9. Sam: Has Noor already agreed, or do we need to wait for an answer before I move across?
  10. Lina: The colleague coordination still needs confirmation. I will ask Noor, but I should not tell you the arrangement has already been accepted.
  11. Sam: Please ask. Directions are enough for the move; I don't need anyone to take hold of the aid.
  12. Lina: Understood. Your assistance boundary is clear. Directions are welcome; touching the mobility aid or taking your bag has not been authorized.
  13. Sam: Thanks for checking with me. It's awkward when someone takes my bag before I can explain what would help.
  14. Lina: I will seek permission before any different kind of help. Your answer about directions should not be treated as agreement to every possible action.
  15. Sam: I'm happy to use lane four once you've checked the queue arrangement. Could you repeat the direction?
  16. Lina: Let me give a route confirmation: use the clear main aisle to the left, toward the open lane four. I will also follow up with Noor about the queue.
  17. Sam: Left, then lane four. You've explained it clearly. You can keep talking to me rather than my companion.
  18. Lina: Of course. I will use direct address and keep discussing the arrangement with you. The practical route and your preference are what we need to clarify.
  19. Sam: Yes, that's what I need: directions, my belongings left with me, and help keeping my place.
  20. Lina: That is accurate. The pending request is the queue arrangement with Noor. Lane four is open, your preferred assistance is clear, and I will not describe the coordination as complete until it is confirmed.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A wider checkout without unwanted help

An associate responds to a shopper's access request.

  1. Associate: Lane four's accessible checkout is open and has wider space. Would that work better for you?
  2. Shopper: Yes, this one feels too narrow with my mobility aid. I can carry my bag myself.
  3. Associate: Of course. Would you prefer directions, or would you like someone to walk with you?
  4. Shopper: Directions, please. I can get there, but I don't want to lose my place in line.
  5. Associate: I'll ask the colleague at four to keep your place while we transfer the transaction.
  6. Shopper: Thank you. Is the route past the display wide enough, or should I go another way?
  7. Associate: The clear customer route runs along this side. I'll point out where it joins lane four.
  8. Shopper: I can see it now. Please leave my bag with me while I move over.
  9. Associate: My colleague is ready at four and will pick up your transaction.
  10. Shopper: Good. I'll go across now with my bag. Thank you for arranging that.
A price query blocks the lane

A cashier explains a delay without disclosing another shopper's purchase.

  1. Cashier: This checkout lane is waiting for a price check. Lane two can take the next customer.
  2. Shopper: I'm next in this queue. Will moving mean I have to start again behind someone else?
  3. Cashier: My colleague is opening it for the next person here, so you can go directly across.
  4. Shopper: What is the customer ahead buying that takes so long to check at the register?
  5. Cashier: We're waiting for a price check; I can't discuss another shopper's purchase.
  6. Shopper: Fair enough. I have a few fragile items; I'd rather keep them in this basket.
  7. Cashier: You can take the basket with you. My colleague will help with checkout at lane two.
  8. Shopper: Should I wait for an announcement, or can I move there once you point me over?
  9. Cashier: You can move now. Lane two is staffed and ready.
  10. Shopper: Thanks. I'll follow the clear aisle rather than cut through the closed lane beside it.
An assistance call with a useful location

An associate requests help for a shopper on the sales floor.

  1. Associate: Assistance call for bedding, please: a customer needs a blue cover from the high shelf.
  2. Colleague: Which item and size do they want? There are several similar packages on that wall.
  3. Associate: It's the queen-size blue cover, code B24, on the top display shelf beside the pillows.
  4. Colleague: I'll come to that location. Have you promised the customer an exact collection time?
  5. Associate: No. I said I was requesting help and would stay here while we wait.
  6. Colleague: Good. Please keep the aisle available; I need room to assess the retrieval properly.
  7. Associate: The customer is waiting by the lower display, and nobody has attempted to climb for it.
  8. Colleague: I'm here now. Could you confirm the selected package with the customer before I begin?
  9. Associate: Yes. They pointed to B24 in queen, not the similar king-size package beside it.
  10. Colleague: I've confirmed that with them. I'll handle the retrieval using the store's equipment and procedures.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A checkout route that works
  1. Read the left-hand route to lane four and have your partner repeat it.
  2. Read the shopper's requested help without adding touching or carrying.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Offer another agreed form of help

A shopper says lane two is too narrow. Open lane six is reached through the clear aisle to the right. The shopper wants spoken directions only. A request to preserve the place in line is awaiting a colleague response.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Associate: "The open alternative is lane ___."

Answer and explanation

D. six

Lane six is the available checkout stated in this new scenario.

2. Shopper: "The clear aisle is to the ___."

Answer and explanation

C. right

Right identifies the supplied direction to the alternative lane.

3. Associate: "The preferred help is spoken ___."

Answer and explanation

B. directions

The shopper requests verbal route information rather than physical assistance.

4. Shopper: "The place-in-line request is still ___."

Answer and explanation

B. pending

The colleague has not yet responded, so the arrangement is unconfirmed.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 07: Managing queues and offering practical assistance
Original fictional case: Shopper Sam uses a mobility aid and says the current checkout is too narrow. Associate Lina knows that accessible checkout lane four is open, reached by the clear main aisle to the left. Sam prefers spoken directions, keeps personal belongings and the mobility aid under their own control, and accepts the move to lane four. Lina will ask colleague Noor to preserve the current place in line during the move; that request is not yet confirmed. No diagnosis, physical assistance, or handling of belongings is requested.
Language workshop: A checkout route that works
Communication goal: Ask what assistance is preferred, offer an available checkout route, and respect individual choices and position in line.
Language guidance: Lane four is open and reached by the supplied main-aisle route. Sam chooses spoken directions and retains control of belongings and the mobility aid. Lina will ask Noor to preserve the place in line, but that coordination is not yet confirmed.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand that this checkout is too narrow for you. / What help would you prefer? / The accessible checkout at lane four is open. / The clear main aisle to the left leads to lane four. / Would spoken directions be helpful? / I will not handle your belongings without your permission. / I will leave your mobility aid under your control. / We can focus on the checkout route and the help you want. / Let me check the next step with you directly. / I will ask Noor to preserve your place during the move. / That request still needs confirmation. / I have not yet confirmed the queue arrangement with Noor. / You prefer directions, without physical assistance. / Is there any part of the route you would like clarified? / Please tell me if you prefer a different form of help. / Lane four is available; I will coordinate the place-in-line request.
Vocabulary: queue: Line of shoppers waiting for service. / place in line: Shopper's position in the service order. / checkout lane: Specific path and counter used for payment. / accessible checkout: Checkout identified as accessible in the supplied store scenario. / main aisle: Principal route through the selling area. / clear route: Route free of the stated obstruction in the scenario. / narrow passage: Space the shopper reports as too restricted for access. / mobility aid: Device a person uses to support movement. / walker: Walking aid providing support through a frame. / wheelchair: Mobility device used for seated movement. / personal belongings: Items owned or carried by the shopper. / preferred assistance: Help the shopper says would be useful. / spoken directions: Verbal explanation of where to go. / physical guidance: Hands-on assistance, not authorized by a general offer to help. / permission: Agreement to a specific action. / independent movement: Shopper moving under their own control. / direct address: Speaking to the shopper rather than only a companion. / companion: Person accompanying the shopper, not automatically their spokesperson. / route confirmation: Check that the proposed path is understood and suitable for the request. / colleague coordination: Communication to arrange the next service step with another worker. / open lane: Checkout currently available for use. / queue transfer: Move between lines while managing the service order. / assistance boundary: Limit on what help the shopper has authorized. / pending request: Asked-for action not yet confirmed or completed.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Handing over a till and an unfinished queryFive dollars below the expected count+

Your goal

Report an unverified cash difference neutrally and transfer ownership of two unfinished checks without inventing a cause.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Five dollars below the expected count

At shift change, cashier Tessa reports an expected drawer balance of $180 and a recorded count of $175. A second check has not occurred, so the recorded difference is $5 below expected with no cause established. A separate price query for item P32 is also open. Incoming supervisor Arun accepts responsibility for both the second balance check and the P32 price query. Neither task is completed during the handover. Tessa must not accuse a colleague, describe a proven loss, or imply that Arun accepting the checks resolves them.

Tessa: Outgoing cashier / Arun: Incoming supervisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the recorded difference?

Answer and explanation

A. $5 below expected

The recorded count of $175 is five dollars below the expected $180.

2. What verification is pending?

Answer and explanation

C. A second balance check

The brief states that the second check has not yet occurred.

3. What does Arun accept?

Answer and explanation

C. Both unfinished checks, not their completed outcomes

Arun takes the next actions without confirming a cause or completing either task.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The arithmetic is $180 expected minus $175 recorded, giving $5 below expected. The second check is pending and the cause is unknown. This is a recorded discrepancy, not proof of theft, a specific error, or a confirmed loss.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
till
Register or cash-handling point used for sales.hand over the till
cash drawer
Compartment holding cash at the checkout.check the cash drawer
expected balance
Amount the relevant record indicates should be present.state the expected balance
recorded count
Amount entered after the initial cash count.report the recorded count
cash difference
Difference between expected and recorded amounts before cause is established.report the cash difference
variance
Difference between a reference figure and an actual or recorded figure.quantify the variance
overage
Amount recorded above the expected balance.distinguish an overage
shortfall
Amount recorded below a specified expected balance.describe a recorded shortfall
recount
Another count to verify the initial result.request a recount
second check
Independent or repeated verification under the store process.complete the second check
reconciliation
Comparison of related records and amounts to explain differences.begin reconciliation
opening float
Cash amount assigned at the start for normal checkout operations.verify the opening float
cash drop
Recorded movement of cash out of a till under store procedures.check the cash-drop record
cash movement
Transfer of cash that should be reflected in relevant records.review cash movements
denomination
Face-value category of notes or coins.check the denomination totals
count sheet
Record used to document counted amounts.review the count sheet
transaction log
Record of sales and related checkout actions.consult the transaction log
unverified discrepancy
Difference not yet confirmed or explained through the required checks.record an unverified discrepancy
established cause
Explanation supported by completed verification.distinguish an established cause
proven loss
Confirmed loss, not established by the initial difference alone.avoid claiming a proven loss
open price query
Unresolved question about an item's price.hand over the open price query
incoming supervisor
Supervisor taking over responsibility for the next shift.brief the incoming supervisor
action owner
Person assigned the next check or follow-up.name the action owner
handover status
Current state of each item at transfer.confirm the handover status

The P32 price query is a separate unfinished item. No evidence links it to the drawer difference. Arun accepts both follow-ups, but acceptance does not complete the recount or resolve the price question. Record both statuses explicitly.

C Notice the language

Neutral detail is stronger than suspicion

A precise handover names the expected figure, the recorded count, the unchecked difference, and the person taking the next action. It should not speculate about a colleague or combine unrelated open matters into an invented explanation. Separate ownership from completion.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the expected figure
The expected drawer balance is $180.
State the recorded figure
The recorded count is $175.
Quantify the difference
That is $5 below expected.
Mark the verification stage
The second check has not occurred yet.
Avoid a cause claim
The cause is not established.
Keep the language neutral
I am reporting a discrepancy, not accusing anyone.
Avoid a proven-loss statement
The initial difference does not establish a confirmed loss.
Separate the other item
There is also an open price query for P32.
Avoid an invented link
I have no verified connection between the two issues.
Assign both checks
Can you take the balance check and the P32 follow-up?
Confirm ownership
I accept responsibility for both follow-ups.
Keep completion separate
Neither check is completed by this handover.
Ask for a read-back
Please repeat the figures and the two pending actions.
Preserve the initial record
Keep the recorded count distinct from any later verified result.
Name the current status
Recount pending; price query open; cause unknown.
Close the handover
The next owner is clear, while both outcomes remain unresolved.
6 language and precision notes
Below versus above
The direction of the difference matters as much as the five-dollar amount.
Recorded versus verified
A written initial count can still await the required second check.
Unknown versus suspicious
Unknown cause is a factual limit, not evidence against a person.
Also
This introduces a separate item without claiming it caused the first issue.
Accept versus complete
Taking responsibility for a task does not mean it has been performed.
Initial versus final
Preserve the original count while documenting any later checked result accurately.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handover is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Expected $180, recorded $175, second check pending, cause unknown.

The correct statement includes both figures, the verification stage, and the limit on causal claims.

2. What is known about P32?

Answer and explanation

A. Its price query is still open.

The price query remains unfinished, with no established connection to the balance difference.

3. Which statement wrongly treats ownership as completion?

Answer and explanation

C. Arun accepted both checks, so both issues are resolved.

Accepting follow-up responsibility does not perform the checks or supply their outcomes.

4. Which calculation matches the facts?

Answer and explanation

B. $180 minus $175 equals $5 below expected.

The expected balance exceeds the recorded count by five dollars, with verification still pending.

E Conversations

Five dollars below the expected count

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Tessa: Outgoing cashier / Arun: Incoming supervisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • unverified discrepancy
  • recorded count
  • proven loss
  • handover status
  • reconciliation
  • second check
  • variance
  • expected balance
  • action owner
  • open price query
  1. Tessa
    Before I hand over, there are two things outstanding: the drawer check and a price query. Can I run through them with you?
  2. Arun
    Yes. What's the 1 on the record? Give me that separately from what you counted.
  3. Tessa
    The record says one hundred eighty. I entered one hundred seventy-five on the count sheet. Nobody's done the second check yet.
  4. Arun
    So the 2 is one seventy-five, not one eighty. We'll keep the original entry for the follow-up.
  5. Tessa
    That's right: five dollars short of the expected figure. I haven't established where the difference comes from.
  6. Arun
    I'll record the 3 as five below expected, with verification still pending. No explanation yet.
  7. Tessa
    Should I put cash lost in the handover? I don't want to make the entry sound more final than it is.
  8. Arun
    Use 4. We have an initial difference, not a finding about a loss or a person.
  9. Tessa
    The separate query is P32. A customer questioned the price, and that hasn't been resolved. I don't know of any link to the count.
  10. Arun
    I'll list the 5 separately. Don't merge the two just because they're in the same handover.
  11. Tessa
    Can you take both follow-ups? I'd rather name one person than leave a note saying someone needs to look at them.
  12. Arun
    Yes, put me as the 6 for both the balance check and P32. I haven't carried them out yet.
  13. Tessa
    I'll leave both marked open, then. The count hasn't been checked twice just because we've repeated the numbers aloud.
  14. Arun
    Correct. The 7 is still to be done through our process. This conversation is the handover.
  15. Tessa
    And I shouldn't say the five dollars definitely disappeared. At the moment, the figures simply don't agree.
  16. Arun
    Exactly. A 8 hasn't been established. Keep the numbers, the direction, and the unfinished check explicit.
  17. Tessa
    Could you read it back before I leave? Especially the five below; I don't want that turned into five over.
  18. Arun
    Expected one eighty, recorded one seventy-five, five below. The 9 is second check pending, P32 query open, both assigned to me.
  19. Tessa
    That's everything. No established cause and no known connection between the two matters. The initial count stays in the record.
  20. Arun
    I'll take the 10 forward and follow up P32 separately. Ownership is clear; the outcomes are still open.

Answers and explanations
  1. expected balance Turn 2. The expected balance is the reference figure, 180 dollars, rather than the initial recorded count.
  2. recorded count Turn 4. The 175-dollar count is the initial recorded figure and still needs the second check.
  3. variance Turn 6. The variance is five dollars below expected; the arithmetic does not identify its cause.
  4. unverified discrepancy Turn 8. An unverified discrepancy records a difference without claiming a confirmed loss or alleging misconduct.
  5. open price query Turn 10. P32 is a separate unfinished price matter; there is no evidence connecting it to the drawer difference.
  6. action owner Turn 12. Arun accepts responsibility for the two next actions; neither outcome is complete at handover.
  7. second check Turn 14. The second check is the pending verification work, not this verbal read-back of the original figures.
  8. proven loss Turn 16. A proven loss is a supported conclusion that the initial difference alone cannot establish.
  9. handover status Turn 18. The handover preserves both open checks, Arun's ownership, and the absence of an established cause.
  10. reconciliation Turn 20. Reconciliation compares cash and relevant records to investigate a difference; that work remains ahead after the handover.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Tessa: Before I hand over, there are two things outstanding: the drawer check and a price query. Can I run through them with you?
  2. Arun: Yes. What's the expected balance on the record? Give me that separately from what you counted.
  3. Tessa: The record says one hundred eighty. I entered one hundred seventy-five on the count sheet. Nobody's done the second check yet.
  4. Arun: So the recorded count is one seventy-five, not one eighty. We'll keep the original entry for the follow-up.
  5. Tessa: That's right: five dollars short of the expected figure. I haven't established where the difference comes from.
  6. Arun: I'll record the variance as five below expected, with verification still pending. No explanation yet.
  7. Tessa: Should I put cash lost in the handover? I don't want to make the entry sound more final than it is.
  8. Arun: Use unverified discrepancy. We have an initial difference, not a finding about a loss or a person.
  9. Tessa: The separate query is P32. A customer questioned the price, and that hasn't been resolved. I don't know of any link to the count.
  10. Arun: I'll list the open price query separately. Don't merge the two just because they're in the same handover.
  11. Tessa: Can you take both follow-ups? I'd rather name one person than leave a note saying someone needs to look at them.
  12. Arun: Yes, put me as the action owner for both the balance check and P32. I haven't carried them out yet.
  13. Tessa: I'll leave both marked open, then. The count hasn't been checked twice just because we've repeated the numbers aloud.
  14. Arun: Correct. The second check is still to be done through our process. This conversation is the handover.
  15. Tessa: And I shouldn't say the five dollars definitely disappeared. At the moment, the figures simply don't agree.
  16. Arun: Exactly. A proven loss hasn't been established. Keep the numbers, the direction, and the unfinished check explicit.
  17. Tessa: Could you read it back before I leave? Especially the five below; I don't want that turned into five over.
  18. Arun: Expected one eighty, recorded one seventy-five, five below. The handover status is second check pending, P32 query open, both assigned to me.
  19. Tessa: That's everything. No established cause and no known connection between the two matters. The initial count stays in the record.
  20. Arun: I'll take the reconciliation forward and follow up P32 separately. Ownership is clear; the outcomes are still open.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A five-dollar difference needs checking

An outgoing cashier gives a shift-change report.

  1. Cashier: The first count is one-seventy-five against an expected one-eighty. The second check hasn't happened yet.
  2. Supervisor: That's a five-dollar cash discrepancy. Have we found an explanation, or does it still need checking?
  3. Cashier: Still unknown. I haven't attributed it to a sale, the till float, or any person.
  4. Supervisor: I'll take the second check through our cash-handling procedure. Is anything else waiting on this shift?
  5. Cashier: A customer price query for item P32 is open. The shelf label still needs confirmation.
  6. Supervisor: I'll take that separately. Please give me the item reference and the customer's stated price.
  7. Cashier: They expected twelve; the screen showed fourteen. No payment was taken while the query remained open.
  8. Supervisor: I've got it. I'll review the cash count and contact the floor colleague about P32.
  9. Cashier: Can you confirm both follow-ups are yours before I leave the counter?
  10. Supervisor: Confirmed. I'll record each result in the shift log rather than combine two unrelated issues.
The starting float is not sales revenue

A new cashier asks about a shift report.

  1. Cashier: The drawer started with forty dollars. Is everything in it now counted as sales?
  2. Supervisor: No. The till float is starting cash for change, so it isn't money from today's purchases.
  3. Cashier: I see the forty recorded here. I should keep that amount separate when describing the shift.
  4. Supervisor: Yes. Keep the float separate from sales on this report.
  5. Cashier: There's also a void for a mug. Does that entry mean the mug was returned after payment?
  6. Supervisor: No. This void cancelled the mug entry before the customer paid.
  7. Cashier: Then I'll refer to the recorded event as a void, not assume there was a refund.
  8. Supervisor: Correct. If a report line is unclear, ask about that transaction reference before changing its description.
  9. Cashier: I'll ask about reference V18 now. Its time overlaps with my break, so I didn't handle it.
  10. Supervisor: I'll check that reference with the colleague who did, and we'll leave its explanation open meanwhile.
An unfinished call needs a named owner

A cashier passes a callback request to the next shift.

  1. Cashier: A shopper called about receipt R82 and asked whether their exchange review had been completed.
  2. Incoming cashier: Do we have an answer, or am I taking over the task of checking it?
  3. Cashier: You're taking over the check. The supervisor hasn't recorded a decision in the shift log yet.
  4. Incoming cashier: I'll ask the supervisor, then update the customer through the store's usual contact channel.
  5. Cashier: Please don't say the exchange is approved just because the shopper has called twice.
  6. Incoming cashier: I won't. What response time was actually offered during the earlier call this afternoon?
  7. Cashier: They were promised an update by five, including if the review remained unfinished then.
  8. Incoming cashier: I've noted five as the update deadline. Is their preferred contact method already recorded?
  9. Cashier: Yes, in the customer record linked to R82; no need to repeat private details here.
  10. Incoming cashier: I'll handle that callback. You can mark the follow-up assigned to me, not resolved yet.

  2. Repeat Arun's two actions: second balance check and the separate P32 price query.

Report a different recorded difference

Expected drawer balance is $240 and the recorded count is $245. A second check is pending and the cause is unknown. Price query Q18 is open. Supervisor Jo accepts both follow-ups.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Cashier: "The recorded count is five dollars ___ expected."

Answer and explanation

B. above

Two hundred forty-five exceeds the expected two hundred forty by five dollars.

2. Supervisor: "The second check is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. pending

The check has not occurred merely because follow-up responsibility is accepted.

3. Cashier: "The open price query is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. Q18

Q18 identifies the separate unresolved price matter in this handover.

4. Supervisor: "The cause remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unknown

The initial arithmetic does not establish why the difference exists.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 08: Handing over a till and an unfinished query
Original fictional case: At shift change, cashier Tessa reports an expected drawer balance of $180 and a recorded count of $175. A second check has not occurred, so the recorded difference is $5 below expected with no cause established. A separate price query for item P32 is also open. Incoming supervisor Arun accepts responsibility for both the second balance check and the P32 price query. Neither task is completed during the handover. Tessa must not accuse a colleague, describe a proven loss, or imply that Arun accepting the checks resolves them.
Language workshop: Five dollars below the expected count
Communication goal: Report an unverified cash difference neutrally and transfer ownership of two unfinished checks without inventing a cause.
Language guidance: The arithmetic is $180 expected minus $175 recorded, giving $5 below expected. The second check is pending and the cause is unknown. This is a recorded discrepancy, not proof of theft, a specific error, or a confirmed loss.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The expected drawer balance is $180. / The recorded count is $175. / That is $5 below expected. / The second check has not occurred yet. / The cause is not established. / I am reporting a discrepancy, not accusing anyone. / The initial difference does not establish a confirmed loss. / There is also an open price query for P32. / I have no verified connection between the two issues. / Can you take the balance check and the P32 follow-up? / I accept responsibility for both follow-ups. / Neither check is completed by this handover. / Please repeat the figures and the two pending actions. / Keep the recorded count distinct from any later verified result. / Recount pending; price query open; cause unknown. / The next owner is clear, while both outcomes remain unresolved.
Vocabulary: till: Register or cash-handling point used for sales. / cash drawer: Compartment holding cash at the checkout. / expected balance: Amount the relevant record indicates should be present. / recorded count: Amount entered after the initial cash count. / cash difference: Difference between expected and recorded amounts before cause is established. / variance: Difference between a reference figure and an actual or recorded figure. / overage: Amount recorded above the expected balance. / shortfall: Amount recorded below a specified expected balance. / recount: Another count to verify the initial result. / second check: Independent or repeated verification under the store process. / reconciliation: Comparison of related records and amounts to explain differences. / opening float: Cash amount assigned at the start for normal checkout operations. / cash drop: Recorded movement of cash out of a till under store procedures. / cash movement: Transfer of cash that should be reflected in relevant records. / denomination: Face-value category of notes or coins. / count sheet: Record used to document counted amounts. / transaction log: Record of sales and related checkout actions. / unverified discrepancy: Difference not yet confirmed or explained through the required checks. / established cause: Explanation supported by completed verification. / proven loss: Confirmed loss, not established by the initial difference alone. / open price query: Unresolved question about an item's price. / incoming supervisor: Supervisor taking over responsibility for the next shift. / action owner: Person assigned the next check or follow-up. / handover status: Current state of each item at transfer.
Speaking task: Read $180 expected and $175 recorded, followed by $5 below expected. Repeat Arun's two actions: second balance check and the separate P32 price query. Read the closing record with cause unknown and both outcomes still open.
Partner: Tessa: Outgoing cashier / Arun: Incoming supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Expected drawer balance is $240 and the recorded count is $245. A second check is pending and the cause is unknown. Price query Q18 is open. Supervisor Jo accepts both follow-ups.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Tessa: Could you read it back before I leave? Especially the five below; I don't want that turned into five over.
Arun: Expected one eighty, recorded one seventy-five, five below. The handover status is second check pending, P32 query open, both assigned to me.
Tessa: That's everything. No established cause and no known connection between the two matters. The initial count stays in the record.
Arun: I'll take the reconciliation forward and follow up P32 separately. Ownership is clear; the outcomes are still open.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Original fictional case: At shift change, cashier Tessa reports an expected drawer balance of $180 and a recorded count of $175. A second check has not occurred, so the recorded difference is $5 below expected with no cause established. A separate price query for item P32 is also open. Incoming supervisor Arun accepts responsibility for both the second balance check and the P32 price query. Neither task is completed during the handover. Tessa must not accuse a colleague, describe a proven loss, or imply that Arun accepting the checks resolves them.
Language workshop: Five dollars below the expected count
Communication goal: Report an unverified cash difference neutrally and transfer ownership of two unfinished checks without inventing a cause.
Language guidance: The arithmetic is $180 expected minus $175 recorded, giving $5 below expected. The second check is pending and the cause is unknown. This is a recorded discrepancy, not proof of theft, a specific error, or a confirmed loss.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The expected drawer balance is $180. / The recorded count is $175. / That is $5 below expected. / The second check has not occurred yet. / The cause is not established. / I am reporting a discrepancy, not accusing anyone. / The initial difference does not establish a confirmed loss. / There is also an open price query for P32. / I have no verified connection between the two issues. / Can you take the balance check and the P32 follow-up? / I accept responsibility for both follow-ups. / Neither check is completed by this handover. / Please repeat the figures and the two pending actions. / Keep the recorded count distinct from any later verified result. / Recount pending; price query open; cause unknown. / The next owner is clear, while both outcomes remain unresolved.
Vocabulary: till: Register or cash-handling point used for sales. / cash drawer: Compartment holding cash at the checkout. / expected balance: Amount the relevant record indicates should be present. / recorded count: Amount entered after the initial cash count. / cash difference: Difference between expected and recorded amounts before cause is established. / variance: Difference between a reference figure and an actual or recorded figure. / overage: Amount recorded above the expected balance. / shortfall: Amount recorded below a specified expected balance. / recount: Another count to verify the initial result. / second check: Independent or repeated verification under the store process. / reconciliation: Comparison of related records and amounts to explain differences. / opening float: Cash amount assigned at the start for normal checkout operations. / cash drop: Recorded movement of cash out of a till under store procedures. / cash movement: Transfer of cash that should be reflected in relevant records. / denomination: Face-value category of notes or coins. / count sheet: Record used to document counted amounts. / transaction log: Record of sales and related checkout actions. / unverified discrepancy: Difference not yet confirmed or explained through the required checks. / established cause: Explanation supported by completed verification. / proven loss: Confirmed loss, not established by the initial difference alone. / open price query: Unresolved question about an item's price. / incoming supervisor: Supervisor taking over responsibility for the next shift. / action owner: Person assigned the next check or follow-up. / handover status: Current state of each item at transfer.
Speaking task: Read $180 expected and $175 recorded, followed by $5 below expected. Repeat Arun's two actions: second balance check and the separate P32 price query. Read the closing record with cause unknown and both outcomes still open.
Partner: Tessa: Outgoing cashier / Arun: Incoming supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Expected drawer balance is $240 and the recorded count is $245. A second check is pending and the cause is unknown. Price query Q18 is open. Supervisor Jo accepts both follow-ups.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Tessa: Could you read it back before I leave? Especially the five below; I don't want that turned into five over.
Arun: Expected one eighty, recorded one seventy-five, five below. The handover status is second check pending, P32 query open, both assigned to me.
Tessa: That's everything. No established cause and no known connection between the two matters. The initial count stays in the record.
Arun: I'll take the reconciliation forward and follow up P32 separately. Ownership is clear; the outcomes are still open.
END REFERENCE

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

One sale, two ways to pay

Explain a completed split-tender receipt without confusing cash handed over, cash retained, sale value, and gift-card balance.

Fictional completed sale: total $42.60, including all charges. The shopper used $15 from a gift card that previously held $20, then handed over $50 cash. The remaining $27.60 was paid in cash and $22.40 change returned. Both payments succeeded. Cashier Inez and supervisor Omar review the receipt; no refund is requested.

Inez: Cashier / Omar: Supervisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • remaining balance
  • outstanding amount
  • cash retained
  • change
  • split tender
  • cash portion
  1. Inez
    Could you check how I'm explaining this receipt? It shows forty-two sixty for the sale, fifteen on a gift card, and fifty cash tendered.
  2. Omar
    That's a 1: one sale, two payment methods. The fifty-dollar note isn't all money retained for the purchase.
  3. Inez
    The customer asked us to use only fifteen from the twenty-dollar gift card. We did that before the cash portion.
  4. Omar
    Then the card's 2 is five dollars. Don't describe the original twenty as the amount redeemed.
  5. Inez
    After applying fifteen, the screen said twenty-seven sixty still due. That is forty-two sixty minus fifteen.
  6. Omar
    Right. That was the 3 of the sale. The customer handed over fifty to cover it.
  7. Inez
    So the difference between the note and that remaining amount is twenty-two forty. We returned that at the counter.
  8. Omar
    Yes, that's the 4. It comes from fifty minus twenty-seven sixty, not fifty minus the whole sale.
  9. Inez
    If I subtract the whole forty-two sixty from fifty, I get seven forty. That would ignore the fifteen already paid on the gift card.
  10. Omar
    Exactly. You'd be collecting that gift-card portion twice. The 5 is twenty-seven sixty, and the gift-card redemption supplies the other fifteen.
  11. Inez
    I can explain that as fifty handed over, twenty-two forty handed back, and twenty-seven sixty applied to the purchase.
  12. Omar
    Good. Both methods succeeded, so the sale's 6 is zero. The five left on the gift card isn't an unpaid part of this sale.
  13. Inez
    What if the customer sees two payment lines and thinks we've charged forty-two sixty twice?
  14. Omar
    Point to the sale total once, then the two portions: fifteen and twenty-seven sixty. Their sum is forty-two sixty, not eighty-five twenty.
  15. Inez
    And the fifty-cash entry records what was handed over, rather than a third payment on top of those portions?
  16. Omar
    Correct. Read it with the change entry. Don't add the tendered note to the net cash portion when explaining the total.
  17. Inez
    Should I call the twenty-two forty a refund? Money did go back to the shopper during the transaction.
  18. Omar
    Call it change. No return or refund is requested here; it was excess cash tendered for this same purchase.
  19. Inez
    Then my summary is one completed sale, fifteen gift-card value used, twenty-seven sixty cash paid, twenty-two forty change, and five still on the gift card.
  20. Omar
    That's clear. Use the receipt and actual confirmed payment results. This example explains the amounts; it doesn't supply buttons or procedures for another checkout system.

Answers and explanations
  1. split tender Turn 2. Split tender means one sale paid using more than one payment method; it does not create two separate sales.
  2. remaining balance Turn 4. Twenty dollars on the gift card minus fifteen redeemed leaves a five-dollar remaining balance.
  3. cash portion Turn 6. The cash portion is the remaining 27.60 paid toward the sale, not the 50.00 note initially handed over.
  4. change Turn 8. Cash tendered of 50.00 minus the 27.60 cash portion produces 22.40 change.
  5. cash retained Turn 10. The store retains 27.60 in cash after returning change; this plus 15.00 redeemed equals the sale total.
  6. outstanding amount Turn 12. Both successful payment portions total 42.60, leaving zero outstanding; the five-dollar gift balance is separate.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Inez: Could you check how I'm explaining this receipt? It shows forty-two sixty for the sale, fifteen on a gift card, and fifty cash tendered.
  2. Omar: That's a split tender: one sale, two payment methods. The fifty-dollar note isn't all money retained for the purchase.
  3. Inez: The customer asked us to use only fifteen from the twenty-dollar gift card. We did that before the cash portion.
  4. Omar: Then the card's remaining balance is five dollars. Don't describe the original twenty as the amount redeemed.
  5. Inez: After applying fifteen, the screen said twenty-seven sixty still due. That is forty-two sixty minus fifteen.
  6. Omar: Right. That was the cash portion of the sale. The customer handed over fifty to cover it.
  7. Inez: So the difference between the note and that remaining amount is twenty-two forty. We returned that at the counter.
  8. Omar: Yes, that's the change. It comes from fifty minus twenty-seven sixty, not fifty minus the whole sale.
  9. Inez: If I subtract the whole forty-two sixty from fifty, I get seven forty. That would ignore the fifteen already paid on the gift card.
  10. Omar: Exactly. You'd be collecting that gift-card portion twice. The cash retained is twenty-seven sixty, and the gift-card redemption supplies the other fifteen.
  11. Inez: I can explain that as fifty handed over, twenty-two forty handed back, and twenty-seven sixty applied to the purchase.
  12. Omar: Good. Both methods succeeded, so the sale's outstanding amount is zero. The five left on the gift card isn't an unpaid part of this sale.
  13. Inez: What if the customer sees two payment lines and thinks we've charged forty-two sixty twice?
  14. Omar: Point to the sale total once, then the two portions: fifteen and twenty-seven sixty. Their sum is forty-two sixty, not eighty-five twenty.
  15. Inez: And the fifty-cash entry records what was handed over, rather than a third payment on top of those portions?
  16. Omar: Correct. Read it with the change entry. Don't add the tendered note to the net cash portion when explaining the total.
  17. Inez: Should I call the twenty-two forty a refund? Money did go back to the shopper during the transaction.
  18. Omar: Call it change. No return or refund is requested here; it was excess cash tendered for this same purchase.
  19. Inez: Then my summary is one completed sale, fifteen gift-card value used, twenty-seven sixty cash paid, twenty-two forty change, and five still on the gift card.
  20. Omar: That's clear. Use the receipt and actual confirmed payment results. This example explains the amounts; it doesn't supply buttons or procedures for another checkout system.
Separate the two remaining balances

A completed $68.25 sale has no additional charges. A gift card held $30; $18 was redeemed. The shopper handed over $60 cash for the remaining $50.25, and the correct change was returned. Both portions succeeded.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Cashier: The cash applied to the sale is $___ .

Answer and explanation

B. 50.25

The sale total 68.25 minus the 18.00 gift-card redemption leaves 50.25 paid in cash.

2. Supervisor: The change returned is $___ .

Answer and explanation

D. 9.75

Sixty dollars tendered minus the 50.25 cash portion gives 9.75 change.

3. Cashier: The gift card still holds $___ .

Answer and explanation

A. 12

The card began with 30 and only 18 was redeemed, leaving 12.

4. Supervisor: The amount still due on this completed sale is ___ .

Answer and explanation

A. zero

The successful 18.00 and 50.25 portions together pay all 68.25; the gift-card balance is separate.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.

Facings are not the full count

Read a shelf plan, calculate a refill, and distinguish moving stock within a store from receiving additional stock.

Fictional shelf plan: SKU B24 has three facings across, four units deep per facing, one unit high. Seven units are on the shelf. The stockroom holds one unopened six-unit case plus five loose units, all available. No other locations, sales, damage, or deliveries occur. Mina and Dev plan to fill the assigned space.

Mina: Retail associate / Dev: Department lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • case pack
  • replenishment quantity
  • location quantities
  • internal transfer
  • shelf capacity
  • facings
  1. Mina
    The shelf looks almost full from the front, but the refill sheet says we're short. Am I counting the wrong thing?
  2. Dev
    Count the actual units, not just the 1. This plan assigns three front positions to B24, each holding four units deep, including the front unit.
  3. Mina
    I counted seven units altogether, including the ones at the back. There aren't four in every position yet.
  4. Dev
    The 2 is twelve: three across, four deep, one high. Seven on hand there means five more will fill the planned space.
  5. Mina
    The stockroom has a sealed case marked six and five loose units. I nearly brought the whole case because it was easier to count.
  6. Dev
    The 3 is five units, not one case. The loose five are enough for this refill.
  7. Mina
    That lets us leave the six-unit case unopened. The shelf would then have twelve and the stockroom would have six.
  8. Dev
    Yes. The 4 is six, but it doesn't set the refill quantity. We fill the assigned space from available stock.
  9. Mina
    Would I add five to the store's total stock when I record that move? The shelf number is going up by five.
  10. Dev
    No. This is an 5 from stockroom to shelf, not a new delivery. Total store stock remains eighteen.
  11. Mina
    Before the move that was seven on the shelf plus eleven in the stockroom. Afterwards it's twelve plus six.
  12. Dev
    Exactly. Keep the 6 separate. A shelf increase can have an equal stockroom decrease.
  13. Mina
    What if I just pull the seven existing units forward so each front position looks neat? Would that count as the refill?
  14. Dev
    It would improve presentation, but it wouldn't add the missing five units. Fronting the stock and replenishing it are different actions.
  15. Mina
    I also see a gap next to B24. Could I spread its three facings over that space to make the area look full?
  16. Dev
    Use the current plan rather than taking another product's allocation. Check the SKU and shelf label so B24 doesn't end up behind the wrong price.
  17. Mina
    So first I use the plan's three positions, then the actual seven-unit count, then move five matching loose units from the stockroom.
  18. Dev
    Yes, following the store's handling and rotation process. The arithmetic alone doesn't authorize changing labels, the layout, or stock condition.
  19. Mina
    I'll record the locations accurately after the move, with twelve on the shelf and six in the stockroom if the count matches.
  20. Dev
    Good. No extra sale or receipt belongs in that record. We've planned a five-unit move within the same eighteen units.

Answers and explanations
  1. facings Turn 2. Facings are the three front positions across the shelf; each can hold additional units behind it.
  2. shelf capacity Turn 4. Three facings multiplied by four units deep and one unit high gives twelve units of assigned shelf capacity.
  3. replenishment quantity Turn 6. The refill is twelve minus seven, or five units; bringing six would exceed the assigned shelf quantity by one.
  4. case pack Turn 8. The case pack contains six individual units; one case is not the same quantity as one unit or the five-unit refill.
  5. internal transfer Turn 10. Moving five units from stockroom to shelf changes their location, not the store's total quantity.
  6. location quantities Turn 12. Location quantities change from seven and eleven to twelve and six, while their combined total stays eighteen.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mina: The shelf looks almost full from the front, but the refill sheet says we're short. Am I counting the wrong thing?
  2. Dev: Count the actual units, not just the facings. This plan assigns three front positions to B24, each holding four units deep, including the front unit.
  3. Mina: I counted seven units altogether, including the ones at the back. There aren't four in every position yet.
  4. Dev: The shelf capacity is twelve: three across, four deep, one high. Seven on hand there means five more will fill the planned space.
  5. Mina: The stockroom has a sealed case marked six and five loose units. I nearly brought the whole case because it was easier to count.
  6. Dev: The replenishment quantity is five units, not one case. The loose five are enough for this refill.
  7. Mina: That lets us leave the six-unit case unopened. The shelf would then have twelve and the stockroom would have six.
  8. Dev: Yes. The case pack is six, but it doesn't set the refill quantity. We fill the assigned space from available stock.
  9. Mina: Would I add five to the store's total stock when I record that move? The shelf number is going up by five.
  10. Dev: No. This is an internal transfer from stockroom to shelf, not a new delivery. Total store stock remains eighteen.
  11. Mina: Before the move that was seven on the shelf plus eleven in the stockroom. Afterwards it's twelve plus six.
  12. Dev: Exactly. Keep the location quantities separate. A shelf increase can have an equal stockroom decrease.
  13. Mina: What if I just pull the seven existing units forward so each front position looks neat? Would that count as the refill?
  14. Dev: It would improve presentation, but it wouldn't add the missing five units. Fronting the stock and replenishing it are different actions.
  15. Mina: I also see a gap next to B24. Could I spread its three facings over that space to make the area look full?
  16. Dev: Use the current plan rather than taking another product's allocation. Check the SKU and shelf label so B24 doesn't end up behind the wrong price.
  17. Mina: So first I use the plan's three positions, then the actual seven-unit count, then move five matching loose units from the stockroom.
  18. Dev: Yes, following the store's handling and rotation process. The arithmetic alone doesn't authorize changing labels, the layout, or stock condition.
  19. Mina: I'll record the locations accurately after the move, with twelve on the shelf and six in the stockroom if the count matches.
  20. Dev: Good. No extra sale or receipt belongs in that record. We've planned a five-unit move within the same eighteen units.
Refill a different shelf

SKU C18 has two facings, five units deep, one high. Four units are on the shelf and nine available units are in the stockroom. Move only enough to fill the assigned shelf. No other stock movements occur.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Associate: The assigned shelf capacity is ___ units.

Answer and explanation

A. ten

Two facings times five units deep times one high equals ten units.

2. Lead: Move ___ units from the stockroom.

Answer and explanation

B. six

Ten capacity minus four already on the shelf gives a six-unit refill.

3. Associate: The stockroom will retain ___ units.

Answer and explanation

B. three

Nine available units minus the six moved leaves three in the stockroom.

4. Lead: Total store stock remains ___ units.

Answer and explanation

D. thirteen

Four plus nine before the move equals ten plus three afterwards; no stock was received or sold.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.

The weight behind the price

Explain gross, tare, and net weight with consistent units, without deducting packaging twice.

Fictional deli label check: filled container gross weight 0.845 kg; correct container tare 0.035 kg; label net weight 0.810 kg. Product price is $12.00 per kg, with no other charges. The label price is $9.72. Shopper Eva and associate Bao compare the label with the weighing record; the tare is already deducted.

Eva: Shopper / Bao: Retail associate

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • net weight
  • difference
  • unit price
  • tare
  • twice
  • gross weight
  1. Eva
    Could you explain this label? I saw eight hundred forty-five grams with the container, but the label says eight hundred ten.
  2. Bao
    The larger number is the 1. It includes the food and container together. The label charges for the food's weight.
  3. Eva
    Then the thirty-five-gram difference is the container? I thought perhaps some of the food had been removed between the two numbers.
  4. Bao
    It's the 2: thirty-five grams for this container. Nothing in this record indicates food was removed.
  5. Eva
    So eight hundred forty-five minus thirty-five gives eight hundred ten grams. That's what the label is using?
  6. Bao
    Yes, the 3 is point eight one zero kilograms, which is eight hundred ten grams.
  7. Eva
    The price says twelve dollars a kilogram. I shouldn't multiply twelve by eight hundred ten, because that's a number in grams.
  8. Bao
    Right. Match the 4 to kilograms: twelve times point eight one gives nine dollars seventy-two.
  9. Eva
    And if the container had been included, twelve times point eight four five would have come to ten dollars fourteen.
  10. Bao
    Yes. The 5 would be forty-two cents. The actual nine-seventy-two label already excludes that container weight.
  11. Eva
    Could you take the thirty-five grams off the label weight now, just to make sure I don't pay for the packaging?
  12. Bao
    That would deduct it 6. The label says net, so its eight hundred ten grams is already the product-only amount.
  13. Eva
    I see. I was comparing a combined weight on the record with a product-only weight on the label, not two measurements of the same thing.
  14. Bao
    Exactly. Keeping gross, tare, and net named makes the difference clear. We shouldn't treat every number on the record as another chargeable weight.
  15. Eva
    Does the same thirty-five-gram deduction apply to every container you use, or just the one in this check?
  16. Bao
    Just the correct container tare supplied here. Different packaging needs its applicable verified tare; we don't carry a convenient number across every container.
  17. Eva
    And this calculation doesn't tell us whether a scale is accurate in general? It only shows how these recorded numbers fit together.
  18. Bao
    That's right. Actual scales and their use need the required checks. This record lets us explain the price, not certify the equipment.
  19. Eva
    Then the food is eight hundred ten grams at twelve dollars a kilogram, and the total here is nine seventy-two.
  20. Bao
    Correct, with no additional charges in this case. The container was accounted for once, and the units and label price agree.

Answers and explanations
  1. gross weight Turn 2. Gross weight includes product and container; 0.845 kg is not the product-only weight to charge here.
  2. tare Turn 4. Tare is the container weight, 0.035 kg or 35 g, deducted from gross to find net product weight.
  3. net weight Turn 6. Net weight is 0.845 minus 0.035, or 0.810 kg, after the correct tare has already been deducted.
  4. unit price Turn 8. The supplied unit price is twelve dollars per kilogram, so the multiplier must be kilograms rather than grams.
  5. difference Turn 10. 10.14 based on gross minus the correct 9.72 based on net is 0.42, the price attributable to the container weight.
  6. twice Turn 12. Tare has already reduced 0.845 kg to 0.810 kg; subtracting it again would understate the product weight.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Eva: Could you explain this label? I saw eight hundred forty-five grams with the container, but the label says eight hundred ten.
  2. Bao: The larger number is the gross weight. It includes the food and container together. The label charges for the food's weight.
  3. Eva: Then the thirty-five-gram difference is the container? I thought perhaps some of the food had been removed between the two numbers.
  4. Bao: It's the tare: thirty-five grams for this container. Nothing in this record indicates food was removed.
  5. Eva: So eight hundred forty-five minus thirty-five gives eight hundred ten grams. That's what the label is using?
  6. Bao: Yes, the net weight is point eight one zero kilograms, which is eight hundred ten grams.
  7. Eva: The price says twelve dollars a kilogram. I shouldn't multiply twelve by eight hundred ten, because that's a number in grams.
  8. Bao: Right. Match the unit price to kilograms: twelve times point eight one gives nine dollars seventy-two.
  9. Eva: And if the container had been included, twelve times point eight four five would have come to ten dollars fourteen.
  10. Bao: Yes. The difference would be forty-two cents. The actual nine-seventy-two label already excludes that container weight.
  11. Eva: Could you take the thirty-five grams off the label weight now, just to make sure I don't pay for the packaging?
  12. Bao: That would deduct it twice. The label says net, so its eight hundred ten grams is already the product-only amount.
  13. Eva: I see. I was comparing a combined weight on the record with a product-only weight on the label, not two measurements of the same thing.
  14. Bao: Exactly. Keeping gross, tare, and net named makes the difference clear. We shouldn't treat every number on the record as another chargeable weight.
  15. Eva: Does the same thirty-five-gram deduction apply to every container you use, or just the one in this check?
  16. Bao: Just the correct container tare supplied here. Different packaging needs its applicable verified tare; we don't carry a convenient number across every container.
  17. Eva: And this calculation doesn't tell us whether a scale is accurate in general? It only shows how these recorded numbers fit together.
  18. Bao: That's right. Actual scales and their use need the required checks. This record lets us explain the price, not certify the equipment.
  19. Eva: Then the food is eight hundred ten grams at twelve dollars a kilogram, and the total here is nine seventy-two.
  20. Bao: Correct, with no additional charges in this case. The container was accounted for once, and the units and label price agree.
Use the net figure once

Gross weight is 1.260 kg, tare 0.060 kg, and the label already shows net 1.200 kg. The price is $7.50 per kg, with no other charges. A gross-weight calculation would incorrectly give $9.45.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Associate: The labelled net product weight is ___ grams.

Answer and explanation

B. 1200

1.200 kilograms equals 1200 grams after subtracting the sixty-gram tare.

2. Shopper: The correct total is $___ .

Answer and explanation

C. 9.00

1.200 kg multiplied by 7.50 dollars per kg gives 9.00.

3. Associate: Charging on gross weight would add $___ incorrectly.

Answer and explanation

D. 0.45

The incorrect 9.45 exceeds the correct 9.00 by 0.45, attributable to packaging weight.

4. Shopper: The tare must be deducted ___, not again from the net figure.

Answer and explanation

D. once

Net already excludes tare; another deduction would subtract the packaging weight twice.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.
